# Jacobus Houbraken: Study Notes

**Site:** Jacobus Houbraken (December 1698, Dordrecht - 14 November 1780, Amsterdam), Dutch engraver, the son of Arnold Houbraken; he learned the burin from his father, engraved the portraits for his father's *De groote schouburgh* (1718-1721), and then spent sixty years engraving faces: 400+ portraits, most of them in a standard oval folio module of about 37 x 23 cm, many of them the only surviving likeness of the sitter. His great projects were the *Heads of Illustrious Persons of Great Britain* (first parts 1737, bound volumes 1743-1752, with the historian Thomas Birch and the engraver George Vertue, published in London by John and Paul Knapton), the portraits for Jan van Gool's *Nieuwe schouburg* (The Hague, 1750-51), and the portraits for Jan Wagenaar's *Vaderlandsche historie* (1752-59, Amsterdam publisher Isaak Tirion). In his eightieth year, Britannica says, he engraved scenes from Cornelis Troost's comedy *De Ontdekte Schijndeugd* -- though the Fouquet set of Troost scenes was published 1754-1764, so the late dating is unresolved (see leg 1 C7). In November 1769 he engraved the news portrait of Pasquale Paoli after Susanne Caron's pastel, made from life. Only one sentence of his own words survives: the Dutch pencil note on Gravelot's Raleigh frame design, asking for the oval "somewhat smaller, as I have indicated in pencil" (Q1). His life is known chiefly through the encyclopaedia entries, the museum object records, and the 1875 Ver Huell catalogue raisonne, which no leg in this study read end to end.

**Doorway:** Hop from the Bernard Picart study: Picart turned the Amsterdam book into a visual encyclopaedia, assembling the world's religions from second-hand reports into one consistent engraved system; now meet the engraver who worked beside him on the picture bible and then spent sixty years cutting faces instead of ceremonies -- the son who engraved his father's book and then engraved four hundred faces, many of them the only ones left, each one annotated with the painting it came from and the collection that held it. The doorway is the son who engraved his father's book and then engraved four hundred faces, many of them the only ones left.

One-hundred-and-sixty-sixth doorway check in a row. D1 "Jacobus Houbraken (December 1698 - 14 November 1780)" -- HOLDS in substance and FAILS in the letter (the month and year of birth and the death date hold; but the commonly given birth day, 25 December 1698, comes from Thieme-Becker and is incompatible with the Dordrecht baptismal register, which records his baptism on 17 December 1698 -- the exact birth date is unknown, and "December 1698" is the honest statement; the death date 14 November 1780 is the consensus, FR Wikipedia's 4 November 1780 alone being almost certainly a transcription error -- leg 1 C1/C3). D2 "Dutch engraver, the son of Arnold Houbraken; he learned the burin from his father" -- HOLDS in substance and in letter (EN Wikipedia; NPG; ADB; Arnold's registered pupils were Matthijs Balen, Johan Graham, and his son Jacob -- leg 1 F2/F21). ## The life

Jacobus Houbraken was born in Dordrecht in December 1698, the son of the painter, engraver and art writer Arnold Houbraken (1660-1719), and learned engraving from his father (leg 1 F1-F2). Arnold had ten children; of the others, only the sisters Antonina (also an engraver, for an Amsterdam publisher, known for embellishing cityscapes with animals and people) and Christina (also an artist) are named in the sources read (leg 1 F3). The family moved from Dordrecht to Amsterdam when Jacobus was a child -- the traditional date is 1707, modern Arnold scholarship says "around 1709", and a third variant, 1710, is cited from the British Museum in the #542 file but was found in no source read in this run (leg 1 C2; see C557).

From about 1718 he helped his father with the magnum opus, *De groote schouburgh der Nederlantsche konstschilders en schilderessen*, published in three volumes 1718-1721: volume I 1718, volume II 1719 (the year Arnold died, 14 October 1719), volume III posthumously 1721, finished by his widow and children. Jacobus engraved the portraits; Antonina helped with the oval portraits; after Arnold's death Jacobus assisted his mother with the last proofs of the manuscript before publication (leg 1 F6-F7). The *Schouburgh* names or discusses 1,207 artists, is a sequel to Karel van Mander's *Schilder-boeck* (1604), and is listed among the thousand most important works in the Canon of Dutch Literature (leg 1 F8). Arnold's registered pupils were Matthijs Balen, Johan Graham, and his son Jacob -- Jacobus was formally his father's pupil (leg 1 F21).

Houbraken devoted himself almost entirely to portraiture. His models in engraving were Cornelis Cort, Jonas Suyderhoef, Gerard Edelinck and the Visschers, studied from their prints -- not teachers, but the dead masters whose plates he learned from (leg 1 F9; leg 3, Models studied). His international fame came from *The Heads of Illustrious Persons of Great Britain*, with the historian Thomas Birch (1705-1766) and the artist and antiquary George Vertue (1684-1756), published by John and Paul Knapton in London. The portraits were first issued in small sets of four from 1737, then gathered into Birch's compiled biographies 1743-1752; the plates are mostly dated 1737-1750. The *Heads* comprised 108 engraved portrait ovals (some editions up to 120 plates); Houbraken engraved the large majority, Vertue only about seven; the ornamental frames were designed by Hubert-Francois Gravelot (1699-1773) and engraved before the portraits were inserted (leg 1 F10-F11). He worked in Amsterdam throughout, engraving in his workshop there while the London publishers issued the work; plates are signed "J. Houbraken sculps. Amst." with the year (leg 1 F12).

From 1752 to 1759 he worked with the historian Jan Wagenaar (1709-1773) -- and the draughtsman Hendrik Pothoven -- on the 21-part *Vaderlandsche historie* for the Amsterdam publisher Isaak Tirion, annotating his portraits with notes about the original paintings (leg 1 F13). He also engraved the portraits for Jan van Gool's *Nieuwe schouburg der Nederlantsche kunstschilders* (The Hague, 1750-51) (leg 1 F14). He was also a draughtsman and an art collector; his own drawings after paintings -- after Aert Schouman and Jan Maurits Quinkhard, among others -- served as the basis for many portraits (leg 1 F17). He also supplied plates for English histories: Rapin de Thoyras's *History of England* (Knapton, 1743; continuation with Tindal, 1751), and Dutch books such as *Denkbilder der alten Volker* (1744) and *De gedichten* (1751, with Pieter Langendijk, Jan Wandelaar, Jan Punt) (leg 1 F24).

Late in life he engraved genre scenes after Cornelis Troost (1697-1750), including scenes from the comedy *De Ontdekte Schijndeugd* (play by David Lingelbach, 1687), said by Britannica to have been executed in his eightieth year (c.1778); a set of 32 engravings after Troost's paintings and drawings of Amsterdam social life (1725-1750) was published by Pierre Fouquet 1754-1764, most plates by Houbraken and Pieter Tanje (leg 1 F15; see C558). His engraved self-portrait of 1770 was made after a drawing by Hendrik Pothoven dated 1769; an earlier engraved self-portrait is dated 1749 (leg 1 F16). He never left Amsterdam after settling there; he died there on 14 November 1780, aged 81, having worked to the very end. Lippmann: "To the very last he maintained an even level of excellence in his careful and well-considered treatment" (leg 1 F20).

Oeuvre size is reported very differently by different authorities: "more than 400 portrait engravings" (EN Wikipedia; NPG: "engraved 400 portraits"); 441 portraits associated with him at the NPG; "over 600 portraits of his contemporaries" (ADB 1881; RU Wikipedia); "some seven hundred portraits" (Lippmann 1906). Ver Huell's 1875 catalogue raisonne describes only 120 works in full. The numbers count different things -- distinct portrait subjects vs impressions and states vs a selected catalogue -- and no single number is authoritative (leg 1 F18/C4). His portraits are held by the Rijksmuseum, the National Portrait Gallery (441 portraits), the British Museum, the Metropolitan Museum of Art, the Yale Center for British Art (205 objects), the National Gallery of Victoria, the Art Gallery of Ontario, the Georgia Museum of Art, the Morgan Library, and the UK Parliament Heritage Collections (56 results) (leg 1 F19).

Two corrections the legs establish against the brief that set this study up. First, the chain the brief assumed -- Vinkeles studied under Punt, who was Houbraken's pupil -- fails at its second link: Vinkeles did study ten years with Punt, but Punt's teachers were the engraver A. van der Laan and the painter Jacob de Wit, and no source states Punt was Houbraken's pupil. Punt and Houbraken were fellow-engravers on the same books (Wagenaar's Amsterdam history; Punt reproduced Houbraken's Koerten portrait in 1734). The corrected chain is Houbraken to Tanje, and de Wit to Punt to Vinkeles, the lines crossing in Tanje and in the Wagenaar and Troost commissions (leg 3, Headline Finding 1; see C554). Second, there is no "Jacobus the younger": no source names any child of Jacobus Houbraken, and the "son Jacob" in the sources is Jacobus himself, as Arnold's registered pupil (leg 3, Headline Finding 2; see C555). His one documented pupil is Pieter Tanje (1706-1761), who was the pupil of four masters -- Picart, Houbraken, Troost and de Wit (leg 3, Pupils).

Some *Heads* attributions are wrong and known to be so: the Met's "Mary, Queen of Scots" (1738) is after an Isaac Oliver miniature whose sitter's correct identity "remains unknown"; the British Museum's "Catherine of Aragon" (1743) is actually Louise de Savoie after Holbein. The prints preserved the misidentification for centuries (leg 1 F23; see C560). And the dealer copy that claims Houbraken "taught drawing and engraving at the Amsterdam Drawing Academy", that "his students included many notable artists", and that he "wrote several books on art, including a biography of his father" has no corroboration in any scholarly source found; the only documented teaching relationship is the reverse -- he was his father's registered pupil. Those claims are not used in this study (leg 1 C9; leg 3, Headline Finding 2; see C560).

## M1. The palm-driven burin line

The burin is a four-sided steel bar, square or rhomboidal in section, cut obliquely to a sharp point at one angle, set in a wooden handle that rests against the engraver's palm. Held at a sharp angle to the copper, it is pushed forward by palm pressure; the line swells as pressure deepens the furrow and tapers to a fine point as the burin lifts. Depth of cut = blackness in the print: deep furrows hold more ink and print black, shallow ones grey. The burin raises a burr of displaced metal on either side of the furrow, which must be scraped off (scraper) and the surface re-polished (burnisher), or the line prints ragged. -- Lippmann, Technique of Engraving, pp.1-3. *Houbraken's whole reputation rests on control of this one variable: pressure over distance.* **Takeaway:** the line is a pressure trace, not a drawn mark -- blackness is depth, and depth is the palm.

## M2. Etching as underdrawing, burin as finish (mixed intaglio)

Most *Heads* plates are catalogued as "etching and engraving". The working sequence: the plate is coated with etching-ground (wax/resin/asphaltum/mastic), smoked black, drawn on with the etching-needle, then bitten in acid (aquafortis) -- the etched line stays even in thickness and does not taper. The etched lines are then worked over and deepened with the burin, sometimes until no etching remains visible. Lippmann's rule of thumb: flesh, sky and water in burin; earth and background in etching; or etching used purely as a guide for the burin. -- Lippmann pp.4-6; Met/BM medium fields ("Etching and engraving"). *This is why Houbraken's plates read as both drawn and carved: the free etched line carries the likeness, the burin carries the tone.* **Takeaway:** two tools, one plate -- the etch sketches, the burin finishes, and the finish can erase the sketch entirely.

## M3. Flesh and hair as line systems (the Morghen mechanism)

The praise that followed Houbraken for a century was specific: he imitated flesh and hair "by means of the graver". Mechanically this means two different hatching systems on one face: flesh is built from short, soft, widely spaced parallel strokes that follow the curvature of cheek, forehead and chin and stop short of a hard contour, so the skin reads as a continuous tone with no outline; hair is built from long, individual, curving strokes that follow the fall of each lock, denser in shadow, sparser at the highlight, so each hair is separately legible. The contrast between the two systems -- soft tone vs. legible strand -- is the technical signature. -- Morghen remark via geerts.com/1stDibs; Lippmann p.155 ("technique soft and distinct"); ADB. **Takeaway:** one face, two alphabets -- the skin is tone without outline, the hair is strand without tone, and the contrast is the likeness.

## M4. The standardised oval portrait module

Across hundreds of plates Houbraken repeats one module: a bust in an oval frame, plate roughly 37 x 23 cm (e.g. Mary Queen of Scots plate 37.3 x 23.3 cm; Catherine Howard sheet 35.7 x 21.3 cm; Newton 37.8 x 23.4 cm). The oval crops the sitter at chest level, centres the face, and leaves a fixed margin below for lettering. The module is a production system: same plate size, same framing, same lettering position, so a new sitter can be slotted into the machine without redesigning the plate. -- Met/BM/NGV dimensions; NPG set listing. Leg 2's verified plate marks confirm the discipline: across Bacon, Walsingham, Raleigh and Dudley the plates vary by less than 2 cm in either direction. **Takeaway:** the portrait is a slot in a machine -- the format never moves, only the face changes.

## M5. The emblematic pedestal: biography as objects

In the *Heads*, below the oval sits a pedestal/cartouche carrying small engraved objects that summarise the sitter's life -- a visual CV. The Raleigh plate (1739): the oval is surmounted by a banderole with the name "Ralegh"; below are a book, baton, anchor and globe (author, soldier, explorer), and in the foreground the axe that beheaded him and his severed head, which his wife Bess was said to have kept. The frame tells the life; the portrait tells the face. -- Cooper Hewitt; BM Raleigh record (1841,1211.97). **Takeaway:** the life is told twice -- once in the face, once in the objects underneath it.

## M6. Frames engraved before portraits (the two-pass plate)

Gravelot's ornamental frames were engraved first, and the portrait was inserted into the reserved oval afterwards. The division of labour is visible in the surviving Gravelot drawing for Raleigh: the oval was left blank by Gravelot, and Houbraken wrote in Dutch in the blank and drew a graphite correction shrinking the oval. The plate is therefore a composite of two hands and two cities, assembled in sequence. -- Cooper Hewitt; 1stDibs dealer note ("ornamental surroundings... engraved prior to the portraits"). **Takeaway:** the frame comes first and the face second -- the plate is assembled, not drawn.

## M7. The lettered margin as scholarly apparatus

Under the portrait Houbraken engraves, in small capitals, a subtitle stating the subject's claim to fame, and beneath that, in smaller letters, his own notes about the original oil portrait: who painted it ("B. van der Helst pinxit", "H. Pothoven del."), who owned it, where it hung ("after the Original Painting, in which it appears as Kapitein der Burgerij, based on the Court Martial Chamber of the City Hall in Amsterdam"). The margin turns a picture into a cited source -- the print records the provenance of the painting it copies. -- EN Wikipedia (Legacy, with examples); Met/BM inscriptions ("J. Houbraken sculps. Amst. 1743", "In the Collection of the... Earl of Burlington"). **Takeaway:** the print is a catalogue entry that happens to be a picture -- the margin cites the painting the way a footnote cites a book.

## M8. The reproductive chain (painting -> drawing -> plate)

Houbraken almost never worked from life for the *Heads*: the chain is oil painting or miniature (often centuries old, in a named collection) -> sometimes an intermediary drawing by himself or by Pothoven/Schouman/Quinkhard -> engraved copper plate in Amsterdam -> printed impression shipped to London -> bound into Birch's biographies. Each link can introduce error (see F23: wrong sitters preserved for centuries), and each link is credited in the lettering ("pinxit / del. / sculpsit"). -- Met/BM/NPG records; ADB. **Takeaway:** the likeness is a relay, not a sitting -- and every handoff is signed, so the error is signed too.

## M9. The correction loop across the Channel

The Gravelot Raleigh drawing is the surviving evidence of a live editorial loop: Gravelot finishes the frame design in London, ships the sheet to Amsterdam; Houbraken writes in the blank oval, in Dutch, asking for the oval "somewhat smaller, as I have indicated in pencil" ("als t niet te veel maeyten / is, had ik liever 't oval wat kleynder / gelyk met potlood, aangewezen, heb"), and draws the correction in graphite along the interior arc; the final engraving shows the smaller oval. Design is negotiated, not dictated -- the engraver edits the designer. -- Cooper Hewitt (with transcription and translation note). **Takeaway:** the engraver is an editor, not a hand -- he writes back to London in pencil and the plate obeys him.

## M10. The family workshop and the oval-portrait assembly line

The *Schouburgh* was a household production: Arnold wrote and drew, Jacobus engraved, his sister Antonina helped with the oval portraits, and after Arnold's death his widow and children patched the manuscript together for publication. The oval portraits in the *Schouburgh* are grouped -- three or four small busts to a plate (e.g. plate A: Erasmus, David Joris, Johan Snellings) -- a batching device: several lives illustrated per copper plate, keeping the three-volume book affordable and on schedule. -- Schouburgh article (Biases; Contents plates A-R); EN Wikipedia. **Takeaway:** the book is a household's output -- several lives per plate, because copper costs money and the family is the firm.

## M11. The part-issue subscription machine

The *Heads* were first issued from 1737 in portfolios of four portraits, sold by subscription through the Knaptons at the Crown in Ludgate Street, and only later (1743-1752) gathered into Birch's two-volume biographies with a printed list of subscribers. The NPG holds a set of 67 portraits still in their original part-issue wrappers -- the distribution format preserved. The economics shape the art: steady small batches, a fixed format, and a subscriber list as social proof. -- Cooper Hewitt; BM via Prints and Principles ("Initially issued in portfolios of four portraits from 1737 onwards"); NPG set page (67 portraits in original wrappers); Sworders (list of subscribers). **Takeaway:** the series is sold four faces at a time -- the batch is the unit, and the subscriber list is the audience made visible.

## M12. Stipple, dry-point and proof states as tone controls

Beyond line engraving, Houbraken's toolkit included stipple (dots beaten into the plate with a punch, denser in shadow -- one 1738 portrait is catalogued "stipple and line engraving") and dry-point (a needle that scratches fine lines and leaves a burr, used to lay in light outlines as a guide). Plates exist in multiple states: Princeton holds two states of the Earl of Orkney portrait, one a final state and one earlier, without lettering and with the face "etched only in light outlines" -- the plate was proved, corrected and re-proved before lettering was cut. -- Meisterdrucke listing (stipple and line, 1738); Lippmann pp.3-4, 6-7 (dry-point, stipple punch, roulette); Princeton Graphic Arts Collection note. **Takeaway:** the plate is proved before it is finished -- the state is a draft the public was never meant to see, and the lettering is cut last.

## M13. The late genre turn: from bust to stage

At eighty, Houbraken leaves the single bust and engraves multi-figure comedy scenes after Troost -- crowded interiors, gestures, props, several actors in one frame, with captions and verses in Dutch and French below. The mechanism inverts: instead of one face in an oval with objects beneath, the scene itself is the object, and the wit is in the arrangement of bodies (e.g. the *Ambassadeur der Laberlotten* practical joke of 1739, a painted backside out of a window passing for a foreign ambassador). First impressions were hand-coloured and heightened with gold. -- Britannica 1911; Biblio/De Roo listing (32 Troost engravings, Fouquet 1754-1764). **Takeaway:** the oval breaks open at eighty -- the face gives way to the scene, and the joke is staged, not captioned.

## Works

## W1. Portrait of Arnold Houbraken, 1718

Engraving. The frontispiece portrait of the *Schouburgh* (Vol. I): the author painted by himself, engraved by his 19-year-old son Jacob. Location: impressions in the Rijksmuseum and elsewhere; the plate opens Volume I ("Portrait of Arnold Houbraken, by his son Jacob"). Dimensions: NOT VERIFIED. Mechanically notable as the apprentice piece that announces the family business: a son engraving his father, the writer, as the first image of a book about painters. The portrait sets the template Houbraken would repeat for 60 years -- bust in an oval, name and claim below -- and it is also a filial advertisement: the boy who could cut his father's face in copper.

## W2. Desiderius Erasmus (with David Joris), 1718

Line engraving, 17.7 x 11.7 cm -- REPORTED (Geerts.com, reproducing NPG data). *Schouburgh* Vol. I, plate A, p. 16. Location: National Portrait Gallery, London (impression). The small plate size is the early signature: three heads share one plate (Erasmus, David Joris, Johan Snellings), each a small oval bust, so the page reads as a crowded gallery rather than a single monument. Erasmus opens Volume I because he is the book's chronological starting point (born 1466) -- Houbraken's first engraved celebrity is a scholar he never saw, copied from an older image, which is the whole method of the early years: likeness by transmission, not by sitting.

## W3. Rembrandt van Rijn, 1718

Engraving. *Schouburgh* Vol. I, plate M, p. 272 (shared with Anna Maria Schuurmans and Jacob Bakker). Location: impressions in the Rijksmuseum and other print rooms. Dimensions: NOT VERIFIED. Mechanically notable for what it is not: Houbraken does not imitate Rembrandt's etching manner at all. Where Rembrandt's own etched self-portraits are loose, bitten, and tonal, Houbraken's Rembrandt is a tidy oval bust built from disciplined parallel hatching -- the young engraver translating a painter's face into the clean, reproducible language of the burin, and in doing so making Rembrandt safe for a book.

## W4. Johannes Voorhout, c. 1721

Engraving. From *De groote schouburgh*, Amsterdam 1718-1721. Location: impressions in the Stadsarchief Amsterdam / Rijksmuseum orbit (Wikimedia Commons file sourced to Stadsarchief Amsterdam). Dimensions: NOT VERIFIED. Voorhout (1647-1723) was still alive when the plate was cut, and the portrait is one of the many *Schouburgh* heads that are today, in Wikipedia's words, "in many cases the *only* likenesses left of these people". The mechanism matters more than the face: Houbraken is building a visual archive of a generation that is dying as he engraves it, working from drawn copies by men like Pronk, Quinkhard and Pothoven rather than from life.

## W5. Joanna Koerten Blok, 1718-1721

Engraving. *Schouburgh* Vol. III, pp. 293-308 (Arnold devotes fifteen pages to her). Location: impressions in the Rijksmuseum; the portrait was reproduced by Jan Punt in 1734. Dimensions: NOT VERIFIED. Koerten Blok (1650-1715) was a paper-cutting artist and one of only three women illustrated in the whole *Schouburgh* (with Anna Maria van Schurman and Maria Sibylla Merian). The plate is notable because its sitter is a woman artist famous for cutting portraits with scissors -- Houbraken, cutting her portrait with a burin, is answering one cutting art with another. The mechanism is the same oval-bust formula, but the subject breaks the book's pattern of male painters.

## W6. Portraits of Adam Pijnacker and Allaert van Everdingen, 1729

Etching and engraving, printed from two plates. Two portraits on one sheet: 149 x 103 mm and 197 x 140 mm -- VERIFIED (Rijksmuseum object page, RP-P-2022-1780, read end to end). Inscribed top right "H.D. p. 180". Location: Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam. The two-plate printing is the mechanically interesting fact: two separate portrait plates inked and pulled together, so a single sheet carries two painters at different scales. It shows the 31-year-old Houbraken already thinking like a publisher -- pairing sitters, economising paper, composing a sheet rather than just a plate -- and it is dated 1729, eight years after the *Schouburgh*, i.e. the moment he is moving from his father's book into independent print production.

## W7. George Frideric Handel, c. 1737 (published 1738)

Engraving. After a lost original; the surround and the scene below the portrait are after Hubert-Francois Gravelot. Location: National Portrait Gallery, London (NPG D3213; also D3217 from the same plate); Yale University, Irving S. Gilmore Music Library (Portrait File Box 16). Dimensions: NOT VERIFIED. Yale's catalogue note makes this the most historically loaded plate in the group: Handel "was portrayed more often than any other composer of the eighteenth century, but this portrait... is the only one known to have had his approval". The print was a gift to subscribers of the printed score of the oratorio *Alexander's Feast* (published 1738), and below the bust Houbraken/Gravelot visualise a scene from the oratorio text celebrating music's power to transform feeling. The mechanism is the mature Houbraken formula at full stretch: portrait above, emblematic vignette below, the sitter's achievement told twice -- once in the face, once in the picture underneath.

## W8. Sir Francis Bacon, 1st Viscount St Alban, 1738

Engraving on paper. Sheet 38 x 24.4 cm; plate mark 36.6 x 23 cm -- VERIFIED (Art Gallery of Ontario detail report, read end to end). Location: Art Gallery of Ontario; also National Portrait Gallery, London (NPG D42898, published 1738 by John & Paul Knapton). The plate is almost exactly the standard *Heads* folio size (about 37 x 23 cm), and the AGO sheet is large-margined, which is how these prints were meant to be seen -- a small portrait floating in a generous white field, the way a book plate is seen, not a framed picture. Bacon's head is built from the fine, even cross-hatching Houbraken learned from the French school (Nanteuil, Drevet, Edelinck): the modelling is all in the density and direction of the line, with no etched tone to lean on.

## W9. Sir Walter Raleigh, 1739

Engraving on paper. Sheet 49 x 32.1 cm; plate mark 37.9 x 23.8 cm -- VERIFIED (Art Gallery of Ontario detail report, read end to end). Location: Art Gallery of Ontario; also National Portrait Gallery, London (NPG D42903, published 1739). The sheet is notably larger than the Bacon sheet (49 cm tall against 38 cm) while the plate mark is the same standard size -- a reminder that "dimensions" for these prints means two different things, plate and paper, and that collectors' margins vary wildly between impressions. Raleigh, beheaded in 1618, is another sitter Houbraken never saw; the plate is a copy of a copy, and its authority comes entirely from the steadiness of the burin, not from any encounter. Below the oval, the emblematic pedestal carries the book, baton, anchor and globe -- and the axe, and the severed head (see M5).

## W10. Sir Francis Walsingham, 1738

Engraving on paper. Sheet 47.4 x 31.7 cm; plate mark 37.2 x 23.6 cm; mat 54.6 x 40.6 cm -- VERIFIED (Art Gallery of Ontario detail report, read end to end). Location: Art Gallery of Ontario. Dated 1738-1758 in the AGO record (the wide range reflects reissue dating; the NPG *Heads* set places the plate in the 1735-1742 part-issue). The plate mark again sits within a millimetre or two of the house standard, which is itself a finding: across Bacon, Walsingham, Raleigh and Dudley, Houbraken's *Heads* plates vary by less than 2 cm in either direction. The series is a manufactured set, cut to a template, and that template discipline is what let 67 portraits feel like one book.

## W11. Robert Dudley, Earl of Leicester, 1738

Engraving on paper. Sheet 45.3 x 31.5 cm; plate mark 36.5 x 22.5 cm -- VERIFIED (Art Gallery of Ontario detail report, read end to end). Location: Art Gallery of Ontario; also National Portrait Gallery, London (NPG D42889, published 1738). Dudley's plate is the narrowest of the four AGO-verified *Heads* plates (22.5 cm against 23-23.8 cm), a small but real variation inside the standard format -- the oval is squeezed a fraction tighter, the shoulders cropped a fraction closer. It is the kind of difference you only see by tabulating plate marks, and it suggests the plates were cut freehand to a nominal size rather than ruled to an exact one.

## W12. Sir Isaac Newton, c. 1740 (dated 1742 in some records), after Sir Godfrey Kneller

Line engraving. 14.5 x 9.1875 in (36.8 x 23.3 cm) -- VERIFIED (V&A object page E.3632-1923, read end to end; dimensions "taken from 1923 accession register"). Location: Victoria and Albert Museum, London (bequeathed by Mrs F. B. Haines); also National Portrait Gallery, London (NPG D42883, "circa 1740"). Inscribed "J. Houbraken sculpsit. G. Kneller pinx." -- the lettering itself is part of the work: engraver and painter credited in Latin on the plate, a signature of the reproductive print trade. Newton after Kneller is the most famous plate in the series, and the mechanism is pure translation: Kneller's painted flesh becomes Houbraken's graded hatching, the white wig a field of fine parallel strokes, the dark coat a dense cross-hatch that holds the face forward. A generation later Raphael Morghen would say no engraver ever equalled Houbraken "in the manner of imitating the flesh and the hair by means of the graver" -- this plate is the evidence cited for that claim.

## W13. Catherine Howard, after Hans Holbein the Younger

Engraving on paper. Height 14 in, width 8.625 in (35.6 x 21.9 cm), cut to -- VERIFIED (V&A object page E.2415-1932, read end to end; dimensions from the 1932 accessions register). Location: Victoria and Albert Museum, London (given by Professor E. W. Tristram); also National Portrait Gallery, London. The V&A physical description is the most visually specific museum text found in this leg: "She is depicted in a sculptural oval surround with a putto seated below." That putto is the Houbraken signature device at its most decorative -- the oval is no longer a plain frame but a carved stone cartouche, and a small child sits beneath it like an ornament on a monument. The plate shows how far the *Heads* formula had travelled from the plain ovals of the *Schouburgh*: the frame itself has become sculpture, and the sitter a statue inside it.

## W14. William Shakespeare, 1747

Line engraving, 37.2 x 23.4 cm -- REPORTED (Geerts.com, reproducing NPG data). Location: National Portrait Gallery, London. Shakespeare is the late *Heads* plate (1747, near the end of the 1743-1752 publication run) and the most copied image in the whole set -- the Chandos-derived head, standardised by Houbraken into the version of Shakespeare the 18th century actually looked at. The plate mark is again the house size, to the millimetre, nine years after Bacon: the template never moved. What changed is the confidence: by 1747 Houbraken is cutting the most famous face in English literature with the same even hand he used on minor Dutch painters in 1718, and the evenness is the point -- every sitter, king or poet, gets the same grave, measured treatment.

## W15. Alexander Pope, 1747, after Arthur Pond

Line engraving. Location: National Portrait Gallery, London (NPG D27577); also La Salle University Art Museum (two impressions listed, 1747 and 1735). Dimensions: NOT VERIFIED. Pope is the literary companion to the Shakespeare plate -- same year, same series, same publisher (John & Paul Knapton) -- and the La Salle list's double dating (1735 and 1747 impressions) is a useful warning that *Heads* plates were reissued and re-dated, so a single year on a single impression is never the whole story. Pope's hunched, sharp-featured head is one of the plates where Houbraken's hatching has to do character work, not just modelling: the satirist's face is all angles, and the burin follows them.

## W16. Oliver Cromwell, 1730s, after Samuel Cooper

Engraving. Location: National Portrait Gallery, London (NPG D42874, *Heads* set); also La Salle University Art Museum (c. 1739). Dimensions: NOT VERIFIED. Cromwell after Cooper's miniature is a double translation -- a tiny painted miniature enlarged to a folio engraving -- and the plate shows Houbraken's core skill in its purest form: taking a likeness made in another medium, at another scale, and re-cutting it so it reads as if it had always been an engraving. The wart, the plain collar, the military severity all survive the enlargement because the line, not the tone, carries the information.

## W17. Anne, Princess of Orange, 1750, after Hendrik Pothoven

Line engraving, 36.4 x 23.2 cm -- REPORTED (Geerts.com, reproducing NPG data). Location: National Portrait Gallery, London. Anne (1709-1759), daughter of George II, married Willem IV in 1734 and became Regent of the Netherlands after his death; Houbraken engraved her after a portrait drawn by Hendrik Pothoven, one of his regular draughtsmen-copyists. The plate belongs to the Dutch side of his career running parallel to the English *Heads* -- the same oval, the same subtitle, but a living sitter, a contemporary, and a political subject rather than a historical one. It is also a reminder of the workshop system: Houbraken rarely drew from life himself; Pothoven, Quinkhard, Pronk and Schouman made the drawn copies, and Houbraken cut them.

## W18. De Ontdekte Schijndeugd: Bely en Dirk, 1754-1764, after Cornelis Troost

Engraving. Location: Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam. Dimensions: NOT VERIFIED. **Dating conflict, flagged honestly:** the Rijksmuseum dates this impression 1754-1764, while the *Encyclopaedia Britannica* (9th/11th ed.) says the *Schijndeugd* scenes were "executed in his eightieth year" -- i.e. c. 1778 -- and calls them "among his best works". Troost's pastels for David Lingelbach's comedy *De Ontdekte Schijndeugd* (1687) were made in 1743, so the engravings post-date the pastels by at least a decade under either dating. What is not in dispute is the subject break: this is not a portrait but a theatre scene -- two characters, Bely and Dirk, in a comic intrigue -- and it is the rare Houbraken where the burin has to stage bodies in space, dialogue, and comic timing rather than a single still face. The scenes after Troost, "the Dutch Hogarth", are the closest Houbraken ever came to genre, and Britannica's praise suggests he was proudest of them late in life.

## W19. Pasquale Paoli, General of the Corsicans, 1769, after Susanne Caron

Etching and engraving, 349 x 248 mm -- VERIFIED (*Rijksmuseum Bulletin* article, fig. 18 caption, in the read range). Published November 1769 (quarto). Location: Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam (inv. no. RP-P-OB-48.413). The most fully documented single work in this leg. Paoli, the Corsican patriot, visited the Dutch Republic in the late summer of 1769 after his defeat by the French; the French-born pastellist Susanne Caron painted him from life (pastel, 300 x 246 mm), and Houbraken engraved her pastel -- "not an exact replica", the *Bulletin* notes, because Houbraken, possibly on Caron's instructions, gave Paoli "a breastplate beneath a more detailed version of the coat in the pastel". Under the portrait run Caron's own French verses, presenting Paoli as a democratic leader and courageous warrior respected by his enemies. Newspapers across Europe claimed this was Paoli's first "true likeness" -- the first portrait made by someone who had actually seen him, after a flood of imagined ones. Mechanically it is the late Houbraken at his most mixed: etching *and* engraving, a quarto format of 349 x 248 mm -- close to the *Heads* folio size, but handled more freely -- and a sitter who is news, not history. The plate sold well enough that a smaller octavo version followed within months (see Beyond the 20).

## W20. Self-portrait, 1769-70, after Hendrik Pothoven

Engraving, 198 x 146 mm -- VERIFIED (*Rijksmuseum Bulletin* article, fig. 2 caption, in the read range). Location: Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam (inv. no. RP-P-OB-48.647). A related impression, "Portrait of Jakob Houbraken, Engraver, 1770", is held by the Philadelphia Museum of Art (object 155393; PMA page read end to end, but it gives no medium or dimensions in the text extract). The *Bulletin* caption records the plate as "Jacob Houbraken after Hendrik Pothoven, Self-Portrait, 1769-70" -- so even his own face reaches the copper through Pothoven's drawing, the same workshop relay as Anne of Orange and Pieter Burman. At 198 x 146 mm it is much smaller than the *Heads* folio plates: the old man presents himself at a modest, almost private scale, in the same year he engraved Paoli. Britannica's verdict on the late work stands as the coda: "the work executed the last year of his life shows no failing of his power in the use of the burin."

## Beyond the 20

- **Portrait of Pascal Paoli (small octavo version), 1770** -- engraving, 166 x 104 mm, Rijksmuseum RP-P-OB-48.414. VERIFIED (Bulletin fig. 19 caption, in the read range). Advertised March 1770 by Martinus de Bruyn at twelve stuivers, to be inserted in part two of Boswell's *Account of Corsica*; this time with Burman's Latin verses and a Dutch translation. Paoli's age, 45, figures in the top corners.
- **Portrait of Pieter Burman, 1759, after Jan Maurits Quinkhard** -- etching and engraving, 266 x 184 mm, Rijksmuseum RP-P-OB-48.872. VERIFIED (Bulletin fig. 3 caption, in the read range). Burman (1713-1778) was the Amsterdam professor whose poem on Paoli Houbraken's circle published; the portrait predates the Paoli affair by a decade.
- **Nicolaas Hasselaer, date unknown** -- engraving on paper, sheet trimmed to platemark 17.3 x 11.5 cm. VERIFIED (AGO detail report, read end to end). A small-format Dutch portrait, close to the early *Schouburgh* scale -- the small oval never left his practice.
- **William Wyndham (Sir William Wyndham, 3rd Bt), undated in AGO record** -- engraving on paper, sheet trimmed to platemark 38.1 x 22.9 cm. VERIFIED (AGO detail report). NPG dates its impression 1739 (published by Knapton); a 1stDibs listing calls an impression c. 1820, plate 360 x 230 mm (reissue).
- **Georges-Louis Leclerc, Comte de Buffon, 1774** -- engraving. Rijksmuseum (via Google Arts & Culture). Dimensions: NOT VERIFIED. A late scientific celebrity, cut when Houbraken was 75.
- **Portrait of Jacob Houbraken, 1749** -- engraving, 308 x 220 mm, Rijksmuseum. REPORTED (Artera, reproducing Rijksmuseum open data). The sitter holds a sheet of paper -- the engraver presenting himself as a man of text and image.
- **Jacobus Hinlopen, 1776** -- engraving, 176 x 118 mm, Rijksmuseum. REPORTED (Artera). A late small-format portrait, four years before his death.
- **Johannes Wesselius, after Jan Maurits Quinkhard** -- engraving and etching on laid paper. Rijksmuseum holds impressions; a dealer listing gives a wide-margined sheet (full dimensions NOT VERIFIED -- the listing text was truncated in search results and the page was not opened).
- **Gustaaf Willem, Baron van Imhoff, 1741-1780** -- engraving and etching, Rijksmuseum RP-P-OB-48.664. Dimensions: NOT VERIFIED (Rijksmuseum page read end to end gives no dimensions in the text extract).
- **Sint Nicolaasfeest (The Feast of St Nicholas), 1761** -- Rijksmuseum. A genre scene, not a portrait -- one of his rare departures from portraiture, after Jan Steen's subject. Dimensions: NOT VERIFIED.
- **Hugo Grotius (Huig de Groot), 1755** -- watercolour, 15.3 x 9.8 cm, National Portrait Gallery, London. REPORTED (Geerts.com). Unusual medium for Houbraken -- a drawing in watercolour rather than an engraving -- and worth flagging as evidence he worked on paper as well as copper.
- **Queen Mary II, 1750, after Gaspar Netscher** -- line engraving, 35.7 x 23.7 cm, NPG. REPORTED (Geerts.com).
- **Jane Seymour, 1746** -- line engraving, 35.7 x 21.8 cm, NPG. REPORTED (Geerts.com).
- **John Fisher, Bishop of Rochester, c. 1737-1742, after Holbein** -- line engraving, NPG D42869.
- **Henry, Prince of Wales, 1738, after Isaac Oliver** -- line engraving, 37.2 x 23.3 cm, NPG. REPORTED (Geerts.com).
- **Anne of Cleves, after Holbein** -- line engraving, 37.7 x 23.1 cm, NPG. REPORTED (Geerts.com). **Dating conflict:** Geerts gives 1730; La Salle gives 1739.
- **Sir Edward Coke, 1741** -- line engraving, 37.5 x 23.5 cm, NPG. REPORTED (Geerts.com).
- **John Maitland, Duke of Lauderdale, 1740, after Sir Peter Lely** -- line engraving, 37.5 x 24 cm, NPG. REPORTED (Geerts.com).
- **Portrait of Maria Sibylla Merian** -- Rijksmuseum (listed among "more by Jacob Houbraken"). One of the three illustrated women of the *Schouburgh*.
- **Portrait of Albertus Seba, 1731-1780** -- Rijksmuseum.
- **Portrait of Michiel van Musscher** -- Rijksmuseum.
- **Portraits of Roelant Savery, Frans Snijders and Theodoor Rombouts** -- Rijksmuseum (a *Schouburgh*-era multi-head plate in the Vol. I manner).
- **Desiderius Erasmus en David Jorisz.** -- Rijksmuseum (the Vol. I plate A pairing, cf. W2).
- **Portraits of Adriaen Brouwer and Jan Lievens** -- pencil and graphite drawing (not an engraving), Rijksmuseum. REPORTED (Artera). Relevant as evidence Houbraken drew as well as engraved.
- **George Frederick Handel, 1768** -- engraving (meisterdrucke listing). Possibly a later reissue/state of the 1738 plate; do not treat as a separate composition without checking.
- **John Pym, Esq., 1738; Sir Francis Drake, c. 1746 (36.5 x 23.2 cm, REPORTED via Geerts); Thomas Howard, 4th Duke of Norfolk, 1735; John Tillotson, 1730s; William Russell, Lord Russell, 1739; Laurence Hyde, 1st Earl of Rochester, 1741; George Savile, 1st Marquess of Halifax, 1740; Queen Anne, 1744; George Abbot, 1740s-50s** -- all *Heads* plates, NPG (some via search snippets only; dimensions where given are REPORTED via Geerts, otherwise NOT VERIFIED).
- **The *Vaderlandsche historie* portraits (1752-59)** -- 21 parts with Jan Wagenaar for publisher Isaak Tirion (spelled Isaac in the Jacobus article -- see C563): unsigned busts in oval, no border inscription, square border, three-line name subscription, after drawn copies by C. Pronk, J. M. Quinkhard, H. Pothoven, A. Schouman and others; proofs before letter exist, and the best lettered impressions bear Tirion's address. (Per Van Someren's 1888 *Beschrijvende catalogus*, via search snippets -- NOT read end to end.)

## Evolution

**Early (1718-1730s, ages 20-35): apprentice and family workman.** The *Schouburgh* portraits (1718-21) are small, grouped ovals -- three or four artists' busts crowded onto one plate, made inside the family workshop under his father's direction, copying older portraits of dead painters he never met. The earliest dated single portrait found is Peter the Great [1726]. The work is reproductive in the narrowest sense: rescue the likeness, fit the module, move on. The Anne of Cleves plate is dated 1730 by geerts.com (1739 by others -- see C7 in leg 1), placing the first *Heads* work at the end of this phase.

**Middle (1737-1752, ages 39-54): the international machine.** The *Heads* is the mature system at full scale: large single plates (~37 x 23 cm), Gravelot's rococo frames, the four-portrait part-issue, London publishers, and a steady output of roughly 100 plates in fifteen years. The portraits are now of the famous dead of another country, after the best originals (Holbein, Van Dyck, Kneller), and the lettering carries provenance. This is the work the NPG and the Met hold in depth, and the work Morghen praised. Contemporary Dutch rivals Pieter Tanje (1706-1761) and Simon Fokke (1712-1784) are dismissed by Lippmann as "merely weak imitators of French and English methods" -- Houbraken, by contrast, is the Dutchman who absorbed the French school without becoming it.

**Late (1750s-1780, ages 52-81): the national historian and the genre coda.** The *Vaderlandsche historie* (1752-59) and Van Gool's *Nieuwe schouburg* (1750-51) return him to Dutch subjects and to the annotation habit (notes on original paintings). The self-portraits of 1749 and 1770 (the latter after Pothoven's 1769 drawing) show a man documenting himself with the same apparatus he applied to others. The Troost comedy scenes -- if Britannica's "eightieth year" is right, c.1778 -- are the only break in the pattern: multi-figure narrative, humour, and (in the Fouquet set) hand-colouring and gold heightening. Lippmann's judgement is that he never declined: "to the very last he maintained an even level of excellence." The output count never falls off a cliff; the subject widens at the end.

**What did NOT change:** the oval, the lettering, the signature "sculps. Amst.", the reliance on painted originals, and the evenness of execution. There is no visible "late style" in the burin work -- the change is in subject (bust to scene) and in scale of project (book to serial to national history), not in hand.

## Disputes and unverified items (flagged)

- **Birth date (leg 1 C1).** 25 December 1698 (Thieme-Becker and most sources) vs baptised 17 December 1698 (Dordrecht baptismal register, per Scheen via the RKD note quoted by NL Wikipedia). A baptism on 17 December cannot follow a birth on 25 December. One of the two dates is wrong; the baptismal register is the primary record and should win, but the exact birth date is therefore unknown -- "December 1698" is the honest statement. (NL Wikipedia's infobox hedges to "Dordrecht, december 1698".)
- **Amsterdam move (leg 1 C2; see C557).** 1707 is the traditional date (Britannica 9th/11th eds.; ADB 1881: "kam 1707 mit seinem Vater"; EN Wikipedia; NPG). Modern scholarship says Arnold moved "around 1709" (EN Wikipedia, "Arnold Houbraken"; geerts.com gives 1709 for Jacobus). The third variant, 1710, is cited from the British Museum in the #542 file; no source read in this run states 1710. Unresolved.
- **Death date (leg 1 C3).** Consensus (EN/NL Wikipedia, ADB, NPG, Britannica) is 14 November 1780. FR Wikipedia alone gives 4 November 1780 -- almost certainly a transcription error (14 to 4), but it is a live discrepancy in a major reference work. A gift-peddling site (giftpals.com) gives 1789, which is plainly wrong and is cited here only as an example of unreliable AI-generated content to ignore.
- **Oeuvre count (leg 1 C4).** "More than 400" (EN Wikipedia, NPG prose), 441 portraits associated at the NPG, "over 600 portraits of his contemporaries" (ADB 1881; RU Wikipedia), "some seven hundred portraits" (Lippmann 1906), and Ver Huell's catalogue describing 120 works in full. These count different things: distinct portrait subjects vs impressions/states vs a selected catalogue. No single number is authoritative; the study says "400+" as the floor and cites the range.
- ***Heads* publication dates (leg 1 C5; see C559).** geerts.com says "published in parts in London between 1734 and 1752"; Cooper Hewitt, the Met and the British Museum say first issued in small sets/portfolios of four beginning 1737, then published as Birch's volumes 1743-1752; Sworders notes plates "almost all dated: 1737-1750"; the NPG set title dates the part-issue 1735-1742. The 1734 date is an outlier with no corroboration found; 1737 (first parts) and 1743-1752 (bound volumes) is the defensible framing.
- ***Vaderlandsche historie* (leg 1 C6).** EN/NL Wikipedia: Houbraken worked on it 1752-59 (21 parts, publisher Isaak Tirion). RU Wikipedia: published 1749-1760 in Amsterdam. Possibly the series began before Houbraken joined it; not verified.
- **Troost scenes (leg 1 C7; see C558).** Britannica says the *De Ontdekte Schijndeugd* scenes were "executed in his eightieth year" (c.1778). The 32-plate Troost collection was published by Fouquet 1754-1764 as first impressions. These may be different series (the Schijndeugd scenes a separate late commission), but no source found distinguishes them; the play itself is by David Lingelbach (1687) -- *not* by Pieter Langendijk, despite Langendijk's name appearing on a 1751 book Houbraken illustrated. Also the Anne of Cleves plate is dated 1730 by geerts.com but 1739/1740 by the Met/NPG -- one of them is wrong.
- **ADB's own error on Arnold (leg 1 C8).** The ADB article gives Arnold Houbraken as born "1669" in Dordrecht; he was born 28 March 1660. The ADB is a 19th-century source and errs on the father's dates -- a warning against treating it as exact on the son's.
- **Unverified dealer claims (leg 1 C9; see C560).** 1stDibs listing text claims Houbraken "taught drawing and engraving at the Amsterdam Drawing Academy", that "his students included many notable artists", and that he "wrote several books on art, including a biography of his father". A targeted search found no corroboration for any of these three claims in any scholarly source; the only documented teaching relationship is the reverse (he was his father's registered pupil). The claims look like invented/AI-generated dealer copy. Do NOT use them in the study.
- **"Mary, Queen of Scots" and "Catherine of Aragon" are misidentifications (leg 1 C10; see C560).** Both sitters are wrong (Met; BM). The study must not repeat the titles as fact without the caveat.
- **The Punt chain (leg 3, Headline Finding 1; see C554).** The brief assumed Punt was Houbraken's pupil. He was not, on any evidence read: his teachers were A. van der Laan and Jacob de Wit. Do not state the link in the study.
- **"Jacobus the younger" (leg 3, Headline Finding 2; see C555).** No evidence found; do not list him as a son or pupil.
- **Mother's and wife's names (leg 3 flags 5-6).** Sara Sasbout (Arnold article) vs "Sara Aletta Souburg" (GenealogieOnline); Christina van Schaik vs Christina van Schaak (same genealogy record). Unresolved spelling variants.
- **Wagenaar birth (leg 3 flag 8).** 25 October 1709 (Wikipedia) vs 21 October 1709 (AbeBooks print title).
- **Edelinck (leg 3 flag 9).** Paris arrival 1666 (Wikipedia) vs 1665 (Britannica); birth "January 20, 1640" (Prabook) vs 20 October 1640 (Wikipedia/Britannica).
- **Cort birth (leg 3 flag 10).** c.1533 vs 1536 (Prabook).
- **Visscher death, Nicolaes I (leg 3 flag 11).** 1679 vs 1709 (some sources).
- **Van der Helst birth (leg 3 flag 12).** 1613 uncertain (Haarlem records lost); birthplace Haarlem vs Amsterdam disputed in old sources.
- **Van Gool name (leg 3 flag 13).** Jan vs Johan (article title "Jan van Gool", URL slug Johan_van_Gool).
- **Levecq spelling (leg 3 flag 14).** Levecq / Leveck / Lavecq / L'Evesque.
- **Vinkeles birth, carried from #543 (leg 3 flag 15).** 12 January 1741 (Wikidata infoboxes) vs 19 June 1741 (BWN/Knoef/bodies); EN Wikipedia article read in leg 3 gives year only.
- **Picture-bible arithmetic, carried from #544 (leg 3 flag 16).** 119 + 27 + 64 = 210, not 214.
- **Quinkhard death day (leg 3 flag 18).** The #542 file gives "28 January 1688 - 11 Novem..." (cut off in the grep window) -- the day 11 November is taken from that file; verify if reused in the study. (See C563.)
- **Suyderhoef training (leg 3 flag 19).** The 18th-century chronological series says "bred under Soutman" -- Soutman = Pieter Soutman (c.1580-1657); not pursued further this run; do not assert without a second source.

## Dimensions verified vs not verified (tally)

Of the 20 documented works (W1-W20): **VERIFIED** (dimensions from a museum or collection page read end to end in leg 2) -- 9: W6 Pijnacker/Everdingen (149 x 103 / 197 x 140 mm, Rijksmuseum), W8 Bacon (plate mark 36.6 x 23 cm, AGO), W9 Raleigh (plate mark 37.9 x 23.8 cm, AGO), W10 Walsingham (plate mark 37.2 x 23.6 cm, AGO), W11 Dudley (plate mark 36.5 x 22.5 cm, AGO), W12 Newton (36.8 x 23.3 cm, V&A), W13 Catherine Howard (35.6 x 21.9 cm, V&A), W19 Paoli (349 x 248 mm, Rijksmuseum Bulletin), W20 self-portrait (198 x 146 mm, Rijksmuseum Bulletin). **REPORTED** (dimensions from a page read end to end, but a secondary source reproducing museum data) -- 3: W2 Erasmus (17.7 x 11.7 cm, Geerts.com reproducing NPG data), W14 Shakespeare (37.2 x 23.4 cm, Geerts.com), W17 Anne, Princess of Orange (36.4 x 23.2 cm, Geerts.com). **NOT VERIFIED** -- 8: W1 Arnold Houbraken, W3 Rembrandt, W4 Voorhout, W5 Koerten Blok, W7 Handel, W15 Pope, W16 Cromwell, W18 Schijndeugd. (See C566: leg 2's summary said 8 verified and 4 reported; the entries themselves give 9 verified and 3 reported.)

## Aesthetic logic

Houbraken's aesthetic is the aesthetic of the record. The oval is not a frame, it is a filing system: one face per slot, one slot per plate, the same size for a king and a poet, the same even hand for a Newton and a minor Dutch painter. The evenness is the argument -- every sitter gets the same grave, measured treatment, because the print is evidence first and a picture second. The lettering is not decoration, it is the citation: the painter, the owner, the room the original hangs in, all engraved in small capitals under the face, so the print can be checked against the world. The emblematic pedestal is the one place the system allows a story -- the book, the baton, the anchor, the globe, the axe -- and even there the story is told as objects, an inventory, not a scene. When the system finally breaks, at eighty, it breaks into theatre: the Troost scenes are the only plates where bodies move, where a joke needs a stage, where the burin has to keep time with dialogue. And the Paoli plate is the system turned to news: a living man, seen by a living painter, engraved within weeks, reissued smaller within months to ride a book. The aesthetic logic is that the engraver is a historian with a burin: his job is to make the face survive, to annotate where it came from, and to keep the tone even enough that the record can be trusted. The Morghen praise -- no engraver ever equalled him in flesh and hair -- is the craft verdict on the same logic: the skin has no outline and the hair has no tone, because the face is built from two line systems that never meet, and the meeting is the likeness.

## Positions

**P1 -- The reproductive engraver as historian.** Houbraken's portraits are not decoration but evidence: in many cases they are the *only* likenesses left of the people depicted (Dutch celebrities of the Golden Age; British worthies whose originals were lost). The print is a historical document first and a picture second. -- EN Wikipedia; NPG.

**P2 -- Dutch tradition plus French elegance (Lippmann's synthesis).** Lippmann places him as the man who "made it his special endeavour to uphold the traditions of his native school of engraving, and at the same time to combine with them the elegance of the French school which was now rising into prominence" -- i.e. Visscher/Suyderhoef's pictorial freedom plus Nanteuil/Drevet/Edelinck's polish. The synthesis is the position: not a French copyist, a Dutchman borrowing French manners. -- Lippmann p.155.

**P3 -- The craft-versus-genius split.** Two verdicts coexist and contradict: Morghen's superlative ("No engraver has ever equaled...") against the ADB's cool "sauber, verstandnissvoll und ganz kunstgerecht ausgefuhrt, es fehlt ihnen nur der Blitz des Genies" (clean, intelligent, fully professional -- only the flash of genius is missing) and its demotion ("they do not reach the artistic height of his great predecessors Goltzius, C. Visscher, Suyderhoef"). Houbraken is simultaneously the greatest technician of flesh and hair and a craftsman without genius. -- ADB; Morghen via geerts.com.

**P4 -- The decline narrative.** Lippmann frames him inside a story of decay: "The changes of taste in Dutch art of the eighteenth century show a swift decline... all the branches of art in Holland at this later period produced only a few isolated artists who had any pretension to special importance. One instance of such isolation is Jacob Houbraken." He is the exception that proves the decline -- the last competent Dutch line-engraver. -- Lippmann pp.154-155.

**P5 -- The portrait as the frontispiece of a life.** In all three of his great projects (Schouburgh, Heads, Vaderlandsche historie) the engraved portrait is physically bound to a written biography. Image and text are one unit: the face authorises the life, the life explains the face, and the emblematic objects below mediate between them. -- Cooper Hewitt; EN Wikipedia.

**P6 -- The engraver as co-author, not copyist.** The Gravelot correction (M9) and the provenance notes (M7) show Houbraken exercising judgement: resizing a frame, choosing which painting to trust, annotating what he saw. The reproductive engraver is an editor of the visual record, and his edits -- including his errors (F23) -- become the record. -- Cooper Hewitt; Met.

**P7 -- Provenance as scholarship.** Houbraken's marginal notes about original paintings make his prints a research tool for art provenance: his oeuvre "form an important record for art provenance" precisely because he recorded where each original hung and who owned it. The print is a catalogue entry that happens to be a picture. -- EN Wikipedia (Legacy).

**P8 -- The family-business model of art history.** The *Schouburgh* was written, drawn, engraved and finished by one household across two generations, with the widow and children completing volume III after the author's death -- and the Schouburgh article notes "it is quite possible that their own opinions slipped into the finished work". Authorship here is collective and domestic, not individual. -- Schouburgh article (Characteristic features).

**P9 -- Dutch technique in the service of British memory.** The *Heads* is a British national project -- British worthies, London publishers, English biographies -- executed entirely in Amsterdam by a Dutchman who never worked in England. The visual memory of Britain's past was manufactured abroad, in a Dutch workshop, from paintings Houbraken mostly never saw in person. -- NPG; Met; Cooper Hewitt.

**P10 -- The late-life genre turn as a coda.** After four decades of busts, the eighty-year-old engraver turns to Troost's crowded comedy scenes -- the only moment in his career where narrative, humour and multiple bodies replace the single face. Read either as a liberation (the technician finally allowed a scene) or as a retreat (the portraitist, his eyesight and market fading, borrowing another man's compositions). Britannica calls them "among his best works"; the ADB counts them merely among "a few historical sheets". -- Britannica 1911; ADB.

## Quotes

*Only Q1 is Houbraken's own words. All others are about him, and are labelled as such. No quote below was invented; each is copied from the source named.*

**Q1 -- Houbraken's own words (handwritten note in Dutch on Gravelot's Raleigh frame design, c.1739):** "als t niet te veel maeyten / is, had ik liever 't oval wat kleynder / gelyk met potlood, aangewezen, heb" -- "if not too much trouble, I would prefer the oval somewhat smaller, as I have indicated in pencil." -- Cooper Hewitt, "Sharp Medicine" (2018), quoting the drawing (Cooper Hewitt collection). *The only surviving words of Houbraken himself found in this research.*

**Q2 -- Raphael Morghen (Italian engraver, 1758-1833), a generation later:** "No engraver has ever equaled, and probably will not equal, the Dutchman Jacobus Houbraken, in the manner of imitating the flesh and the hair by means of the graver." -- Via geerts.com and 1stDibs (both unattributed web text); the original publication of the remark was NOT verified in this research -- treat as widely repeated, not primary-sourced. *Not Houbraken's words.*

**Q3 -- Allgemeine Deutsche Biographie (Wessely, 1881), the national verdict:** "H. war fur Holland das, was Bause fur Deutschland war." ("Houbraken was for Holland what Bause was for Germany.") -- ADB, "Houbraken: Arnold H." *Not Houbraken's words.*

**Q4 -- ADB (Wessely, 1881), the qualified praise:** "M. Houbraken's Blatter sind sauber, verstandnissvoll und ganz kunstgerecht ausgefuhrt, es fehlt ihnen nur der Blitz des Genies." ("Houbraken's sheets are clean, intelligent and fully professionally executed; only the flash of genius is missing.") -- ADB. *Not Houbraken's words.*

**Q5 -- Lippmann, *Engraving and Etching* (1906), p.155:** "His drawing is correct, his technique soft and distinct, the general effect of his prints bright and pleasing." -- Lippmann. *Not Houbraken's words.*

**Q6 -- Lippmann (1906), p.155:** "To the very last he maintained an even level of excellence in his careful and well-considered treatment." -- Lippmann. *Not Houbraken's words.*

**Q7 -- Lippmann (1906), p.155:** "As a portrait engraver, Houbraken was in great request." -- Lippmann. *Not Houbraken's words.*

**Q8 -- Encyclopaedia Britannica, 11th ed. (1911), on his inheritance:** "All that his father, Arnold Houbraken, bequeathed to him was a fine constitution and a pure love for work." -- Britannica (repeated verbatim from the 9th ed.). *Not Houbraken's words.*

**Q9 -- Britannica (1911), on the Amsterdam years:** "In 1707 he came to reside at Amsterdam, where for years he had to struggle incessantly against difficulties." -- Britannica. *Not Houbraken's words; the 1707 date is contested (see C2/C557).*

**Q10 -- National Portrait Gallery (London), person page:** "Jacobus Houbraken was the leading portrait engraver in eighteenth-century Holland." -- NPG. *Not Houbraken's words.*

**Q11 -- Sir Walter Raleigh, quoted in Cooper Hewitt on the Raleigh plate's subject (NOT Houbraken):** Raleigh, examining the executioner's axe before his beheading, called it "a sharp medicine... a physician for all diseases." -- Cooper Hewitt, citing Nichols & Williams, *Sir Walter Raleigh: In Life and Legend* (2011), p.320. *Raleigh's words, included because Houbraken engraved the axe and the severed head into the plate's foreground.*

**Q12 -- Lippmann (1906), p.154, on Houbraken's model Suyderhoef (the technique Houbraken studied):** "He used his burin with absolutely unrestrained freedom, almost like an etching-needle." -- Lippmann. *Not Houbraken's words; included as the technical ideal Houbraken learned from.*

**Q13 -- Britannica (1911), on Troost:** Cornelis Troost was "called by his countrymen the Dutch Hogarth, with, however, very small title to such a distinction." -- Britannica. *Not Houbraken's words.*

**Q14 -- Lippmann (1906), pp.154-155, the decline framing:** "The changes of taste in Dutch art of the eighteenth century show a swift decline. While the remembrance of the great glories of the past was never quite blotted out, all the branches of art in Holland at this later period produced only a few isolated artists who had any pretension to special importance." -- Lippmann. *Not Houbraken's words.*

## Network

Teachers: 1. Arnold Houbraken (28 March 1660 - 14 October 1719) -- father and teacher: "Jacobus was born in Dordrecht, and learned the art of engraving from his father" (EN Wikipedia Jacobus, read end to end in leg 3); Arnold's registered pupils were "Matthijs Balen, Johan Graham, and his son Jacob" (EN Wikipedia Arnold, read end to end in leg 3). Also the Schouburgh author. Counted once here; also family. Proposed as top pick for #546.

Family: 2. Sara Sasbout (mother) -- married Arnold 1685 (EN Wikipedia Arnold, read end to end); Jacobus "assisted his mother with the last proofs of the manuscript before publishing" after Arnold's death (EN Wikipedia Jacobus, read end to end). FLAG: GenealogieOnline gives her name as "Sara Aletta Souburg" -- spelling variant, unresolved. 3. Christina van Schaik / van Schaak (wife) -- married Jacobus 21 June 1733, Amsterdam (GenealogieOnline). FLAG: Schaik vs Schaak. 4. Antonina Houbraken (sister, engraver) -- "His daughter Antonina Houbraken also became an engraver for an Amsterdam publisher, and is known today for her embellishment of cityscapes and buildings with animals and people" (EN Wikipedia Arnold, read end to end). Dates not established in this run. Proposed as extra verified spare for #546. 5. Christina Houbraken (sister, artist) -- "His daughter Christina Houbraken was also an artist" (EN Wikipedia Arnold, read end to end). Dates not established in this run. Proposed as extra verified spare for #546. Note: Arnold had ten children; only Jacobus, Antonina and Christina are named in sources read. The other seven are unnamed -- not counted.

Models studied, NOT teachers: 6. Cornelis Cort (c.1533-1578; Prabook: 1536 - 17 March 1578) -- Flemish engraver; studied under Hieronymus Cock; Venice c.1565 employed by Titian; Rome school; ~150 plates. FLAG: birth c.1533 vs 1536. Proposed as spare 1 for #546. 7. Jonas Suyderhoef (1613 Haarlem - 1686 Haarlem) -- Dutch Golden Age engraver; nephew of Kenau Simonsdochter Hasselaer; engraved Anna Maria van Schurman after Jan Lievens. An 18th-century source calls him "famous engraver, bred under Soutman... worked chiefly after Rubens, Berghem, Terburg, and Ostade... beautiful portraits after Van Dyck and Frans Hals". Proposed as spare 2 for #546. 8. Gerard Edelinck (bapt. 20 October 1640 - 2 April 1707) -- Flemish copper-plate engraver, Paris from 1666, naturalized French 1675; Academie royale 1677; 400+ prints, above all portraits (Le Brun, Rigaud, Champaigne, Nanteuil, Dryden, Descartes). FLAG: Paris arrival 1666 (Wikipedia) vs 1665 (Britannica); Prabook gives birth as "January 20, 1640", apparently an error for October. Proposed as alternate 2 for #546. 9. Claes Jansz. Visscher (1587 - 19 June 1652) -- draughtsman, engraver, mapmaker, publisher; founder of the Visscher mapmaking business; *Leo Belgicus* 1611; ~5,000 prints issued from the shop "Sign of the Fisher" on the Kalverstraat. FLAG: also called Nicolas Joannis Visscher II, after his father Claes Jansz. Visscher I (c.1550-1612). Proposed as spare 3 for #546. 10. Nicolaes Visscher I (25 January 1618 - buried 11 September 1679) -- son of Claes Jansz.; engraver, cartographer, publisher; *Orbis Terrarum Nova et Accuratissima Tabula* 1658. FLAG: some sources give death 1709. Proposed as spare 4 for #546. Houbraken "was influenced by studying the works of Cornelis Cort, Jonas Suyderhoef, Gerard Edelinck and the Visschers" (EN Wikipedia Jacobus, read end to end). None taught him; do not promote them to teachers.

Amsterdam collaborators and book trade: 11. Gerard Hoet (22 August 1648 - 2 December 1733) -- picture-bible co-designer: 119 of the 214 designs are his, against Houbraken's 27 and Picart's 64 (per #544 study, leg 3 read his EN Wikipedia article end to end, 185 lines). FLAG (from #544): 119+27+64 = 210, not 214 -- four designs unattributed in sources read. Already carried as #544 main 2 -- counted in the network, NOT re-proposed. 12. Bernard Picart (11 June 1673 - 8 May 1733) -- picture-bible co-designer, 64 designs (per #544 study); subject of study #544. The Amsterdam engraving line's trunk; Houbraken is his picture-bible collaborator carried into #545. 13. Proposed as main 3 for #546. 18. Apprenticed age 13; seven years under Michael Vandergucht; pupil of Thomas Gibson at Kneller's academy; Rose and Crown Club with Hogarth and Tillemans; official engraver to the Society of Antiquaries 1717, the only engraver made Fellow; ~500 portraits + ~500 antiquarian plates; *Twelve Heads of Poets* 1730; Rapin's *History of England*, Knapton edition 1736 (three years); notebooks bought by Horace Walpole, the basis of *Anecdotes of Painting in England* (1762-71). Proposed as main 1 for #546. 25. Link to Houbraken two ways: Tanje "engraved over 100 plates for Johan van Gool's *Nieuw Schouburg* in 1750, working mostly from portrait sketches by Aart Schouman" (EN Wikipedia Tanje, read end to end), and Houbraken engraved George II "after A. Schouman" and inscribed a plate "A. Schoumen after the Original in the family of J. Houbraken" (EN Wikipedia Jacobus, read end to end). FLAG: Aert vs Aart spelling. Proposed as spare 7 for #546. 26. **Network total: 53 unique persons** -- Teachers 1, Family 4 more, Models studied 5, Amsterdam collaborators/book trade 18, Pupils 1, Engraved-after 10, Sitters 14. (1+4+5+18+1+10+14 = 53.)

Second-order notes (NOT counted in the 53): Arnold's teachers (Jacobus's grandfather-generation): Johannes de Haan (threadtwisting; introduced Arnold to engraving), Willem van Drielenburch (rampjaar 1672), Jacobus Levecq (9 months -- proposed as spare 6 for #546), Samuel van Hoogstraten (4 years -- proposed as spare 5 for #546). De Haan and Drielenburch: dates not established in this run; not proposed. Arnold's registered pupils = Jacobus's fellow-pupils: Matthijs Balen, Johan Graham (extra spares for #546, no dates found). Third generation (Vinkeles's pupils, via Punt): Jacob Ernst Marcus, Jacobus Millies, Abraham Vinkeles, Harmanus Vinkeles (1745-1804), Johannes Vinkeles, Daniel Vrijdag (EN Wikipedia Vinkeles, read end to end in leg 3) -- already covered in #543's candidates; the line runs on past Houbraken. Vinkeles's Paris master: Jacques-Philippe Le Bas (dry-needle etching, 1770-71) -- #543 main 4, carried. Portraitist of Vinkeles: Charles Howard Hodges (lead-image portrait) -- #543 extra spare. Schouman's circle: Adriaen van der Burg (1693-1733, Schouman's master), Frans Greenwood (glass-engraving teacher, inventor of stipple), Cornelis Greenwood -- background only. Ver Huell: A. Ver Huell, *Jacobus Houbraken et son oeuvre* (Arnhem, 1875), "includes a full description of 120 engraved works" (EN Wikipedia Jacobus, read end to end) -- the monograph legs 1-2 should use; noted here, not a network person. NPG tally: National Portrait Gallery lists Houbraken as "Artist associated with 441 portraits" against Wikipedia's "more than 400 portrait engravings" -- the two counts are consistent in scale; use "400+" in the study.

Cross-links worth noting: Houbraken to Picart to Hoet -- the picture bible as a three-way division: Hoet designs 119, Houbraken 27, Picart 64, and the engravers (Gouwen, Duflos, Thomassin, Folkema) cut them all. Houbraken to Gravelot to the Knaptons -- the London frame and the Amsterdam face: Gravelot designs in London, Houbraken corrects in pencil from Amsterdam, the Knaptons sell the result in portfolios of four. Houbraken to Birch to Vertue -- the displaced engraver and the displaced history: Vertue's ~7 plates submerged by Houbraken's ~100, and Birch's lives bound around them both. Houbraken to Tanje to Vinkeles -- the line that does NOT run through Punt: Houbraken teaches Tanje (one of four masters), Tanje engraves with Punt after Troost, Punt teaches Vinkeles, and the school Vinkeles directs for forty-three years is the afterlife of the Amsterdam engraving trade Houbraken worked in. Houbraken to Paoli to the news -- the engraver as reporter: a pastel made from life in the summer of 1769 becomes a quarto engraving in November and an octavo in March, riding Boswell's book.

Influence line: Houbraken's documented influence line runs through his one pupil and through the book trade, not through a style. His single documented pupil, Tanje, carries the Amsterdam portrait-engraving line into the next generation, and the *Heads* themselves are the influence: 108 ovals that fixed what the 18th century thought Shakespeare, Newton, Raleigh and Cromwell looked like, and that are still the standard likenesses in the NPG. No named pupil of Houbraken is found carrying a particular Houbraken mechanism forward by name -- the line continues as a format (the oval, the subtitle, the provenance note) and as a catalogue, not as a style. The documented influence is the portrait as record: his prints "form an important record for art provenance" because he recorded where each original hung and who owned it, and in many cases his plate is the only likeness left.

## Future candidates
Top pick for #546 is Arnold Houbraken (28 March 1660 - 14 October 1719) - the father whose book the son engraved. Arnold taught Jacobus the burin, wrote the *Groote Schouburgh* Jacobus illustrated, and designed 27 of the picture-bible plates beside Hoet's 119; studying him next turns the chain backward to its source -- the painter-biographer whose three volumes made the son's four hundred faces necessary -- before it branches to the London collaborators or the models Houbraken studied. He is the top pick because he is the root of the line this study has been following for five studies: the Amsterdam engraving school's book, the father who wrote it, the son who engraved it, the picture bible the two of them designed beside Picart and Hoet, and the workshop -- wife, son, daughters -- that finished the book after his death. The doorway is the father whose book the son engraved. George Vertue (1684 - 24 July 1756; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 123 lines) - the London engraver Houbraken superseded on the *Heads*. Apprenticed at 13, seven years under Michael Vandergucht, pupil of Thomas Gibson at Kneller's academy; Rose and Crown Club with Hogarth and Tillemans; official engraver to the Society of Antiquaries 1717, the only engraver made Fellow; ~500 portraits + ~500 antiquarian plates; *Twelve Heads of Poets* 1730; Rapin's *History of England* for the Knaptons 1736; his *Heads* contribution "submerged by the popularity of Houbraken's 'brilliant but less truthful productions'"; his forty volumes of notebooks, bought by Walpole, became *Anecdotes of Painting in England*. He is main 1 rather than top pick because he is the man Houbraken displaced, not the man Houbraken came from: the London half of the portrait trade, where Arnold is the Amsterdam root. The doorway is the London engraver whose plates Houbraken's brighter ones displaced. Isaak Tirion (1705 in Utrecht - 1765 in Amsterdam; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 21 lines) - the publisher who turned Houbraken's faces into the national history. *Hedendaagsche historie* and *Vaderlandse Historie*, "illustrated with engravings by leading artists of Amsterdam"; moved to Amsterdam by May 1727; 1742 bought Wetstein's Kalverstraat house; *Het Verheerlykt Nederland* 1745-57, continued by his widow 1773-74. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the trade, not the hand: the man who commissioned the faces, where Arnold is the man who wrote the book that needed them. The doorway is the publisher who turned Houbraken's faces into the national history. Francois Morellon la Cave (15 April 1696 - 9 July 1768; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 39 lines) - the pupil who carried Picart's book-illustration trade into English history publishing. Huguenot engraver, settled The Hague; married Jeanne Bouffet 1722; engraving of Vivaldi 1725; illustrated Voltaire's Henriade and Tragedies, Garcilaso de la Vega's Comentarios Reales (Paris ed. 1731), Rapin de Thoyras's History of England vols I-X (1724-27); engravings after Hogarth and Coypel; portraits of Edward Pococke, Nader Shah, Prince William Duke of Cumberland, Willem de Fesch; active London c.1731-34 and c.1750; self-portrait 1765. He is main 1 rather than top pick because he is the pupil who left the workshop for London, not the collaborator who stayed in it: the trade carried outward, where Houbraken is the line carried forward. The doorway is the pupil who engraved Vivaldi and then illustrated the history of England. Paris shop rue Saint-Jacques under the Phenix (with Gabriel II Martin); The Hague refuge with Picart Jan 1710; abandoned bookselling c.1713; proofreader for Fritsch & Bohm, Rotterdam; editor Journal litteraire 1713-1737; Dictionnaire historique (1758-59, posthumous); London 1726-1735; left his papers to Leiden University Library; with Picart and pupil Jakob van der Schley arranged illustrations for numerous books, supplying descriptions and rough sketches. (The 11 names carried in #543 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Jurriaan Andriessen (1742-1819), Hermanus Numan (1744-1820), Jacques-Philippe Le Bas (1707-1783), Jacob Ernst Marcus (1774-1826) (mains); Izaak Jansz de Wit (1744-1809) (reserve); Hendrik Kobell (1751-1779), Jacques Kuyper (1761-1808) (alternates); spares: Pieter Barbiers II (1749-1842), Jacob Haafner (1754-1809), Jacob Otten Husly (16 November 1738 - 11 January 1796), Jan Bulthuis (1750-1801). Jurriaan Andriessen (1742-1819; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Brabant companion who painted the walls of Amsterdam houses and taught the next engraver in the chain. Wall-covering and theatre-decoration painter; travelled to Brabant with Vinkeles and Izaak Schmidt in 1765; subject, with Schmidt and Vinkeles himself, of the 1765 triple portrait; teacher of Hermanus Numan at the Stadstekenacademie in 1771. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the travelling companion, not the trunk: the painter beside the engraver on the road, whose wall-coverings are the large-scale cousin of Vinkeles's theatre sets. The doorway is the Brabant companion who painted the walls of Amsterdam houses -- and taught the next engraver in the chain. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; the 20 "Andriessen" log mentions are all Louis Andriessen (1939-2021), the composer, studied in the Rzewski chain -- never Jurriaan; no local file; not carried in #522-#543 as a subject. Dates search-verified only (Alamy/WGA captions). Hermanus Numan (1744-1820; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Paris meeting who became the copyist, theorist and publisher of the Amsterdam engraving line. Engraver, copyist, theorist and publisher; met Vinkeles in Paris in 1770; travelled to Paris in 1768, studied engraving there, then studied under Andriessen at the Stadstekenacademie in 1771; later Felix Meritis like Vinkeles and Vrijdag. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the theorist of the line, not its trunk: the man who wrote about engraving as well as engraving, and whose career runs parallel to Vinkeles's from the same Paris meeting. The doorway is the Paris meeting who became the copyist, theorist and publisher of the Amsterdam engraving line. Jacques-Philippe Le Bas (1707-1783; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Paris master whose dry needle Vinkeles crossed Europe to learn, and whose workshop was the largest in Paris. French engraver; head of the largest engraving workshop in Paris; teacher of Cochin, Cathelin, Strange and Ryland, as well as of Vinkeles for a year in 1770-71; Vinkeles lived in his house and learned his dry-needle technique there. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is the French master behind the Dutch pupil: the technique itself, not the Amsterdam line -- and a study of him is a study of Paris, not of the chain that runs through Punt. The doorway is the Paris master whose dry needle Vinkeles crossed Europe to learn -- and whose workshop was the largest in Paris. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#543 as a subject.
