Jacob Ernst Marcus (1774-1826; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the pupil who took Vinkeles's late, loose manner and turned it into a school of his own. Engraver; "one of the few pupils Vinkeles accepted"; also trained in drawing by Steven Goble; gold medal at the Stadstekenacademie 1798; later professor/director of the Akademie van Beeldende Kunsten (1820); founder of the society Kunst Zij Ons Doel with Harmanus Fock and Harmanus Vinkeles; his 106-print Studiebeelden en Fragmenten (1807-16) is the next generation's drawing book; his 1812 etchings portray Jacobus Buys, Izaak Schmidt and Jurriaan Andriessen -- the Vinkeles circle, one generation on. He is main 5 rather than top pick because he is the continuation after Vinkeles, not the trunk before him: the pupil whose handling derives from Vinkeles's late manner, and the proof the school outlived the master. The doorway is the pupil who took Vinkeles's late, loose manner and turned it into a school of his own. (The 13 names carried in #542 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Cornelis Ploos van Amstel (4 January 1726 - 20 December 1798) (mains); Louis Fabritius Dubourg (bapt. 5 July 1693 - buried 22 September 1775) (reserve); Simon Fokke (1712-1784), Jakob van der Schley (26 July 1715 - 7 February 1779) (alternates); spares: Jan Caspar Philips (1690-1775), Pieter Barbiers I (bapt. 23 April 1717 - 7 September 1780), Jan Baptist Xavery (Antwerp 1697 - 1742, The Hague), Gerard Joseph Xavery (27 July 1700 - after 1747), Jacob Folkema (1692-1767), Jan de Beijer (24 September 1703 - c.1780), Jacob van Huysum (bapt. 25 February 1688 - 1740), Caspar Jacobsz Philips (1732-1789). Cornelis Ploos van Amstel (4 January 1726 - 20 December 1798) - the lumber merchant who married Troost's daughter, collected five thousand drawings, and invented a way to print them a century before photography. Pupil of Norbert van Bloemen and of Jacobus Buys (carried #541 main); married Elisabeth Troost, daughter of Cornelis Troost (carried #541 main); a lumber merchant by trade and a collector of 5,000+ drawings including Rembrandts. He invented the prenttekening -- a transfer/offset method of reproducing drawings in the crayon manner, from 1765; his Hoofdwerk ran to 46 prints at 350 impressions each. His collection sold in 1800 for 109,406 guilders. His pupils included Christina Chalon, William V Prince of Orange, Elisabeth van Woensel and Hermanus Petrus Schouten; he reported Petrus Camper's facial-angle findings; a member of Arte et Amicitia. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is the collector's answer to the engraver's question: not the man who made the prints, but the man who figured out how to print the drawings themselves, and the proof that the print trade had a second half Punt never touched. The doorway is the lumber merchant who married Troost's daughter, collected five thousand drawings, and invented a way to print them a century before photography. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#542 as a subject. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. He appears in the #541 file only as network/provenance (leg-2 note, "ex Cornelis Ploos van Amstel collection"; patrons entry 27), never as a candidate. FLAG: one source gives "Print made by: Cornelis Ploos van Amstel in 1559-1760" -- an obvious typo for 1759-1760. Louis Fabritius Dubourg (bapt. 5 July 1693 - buried 22 September 1775) - the history painter who swapped the brush for the burin and taught the academy circle to etch. History painter who became an engraver; pupil of Gerard de Lairesse (carried #529 spare), Gerrit Rademaker (1672-1711) and Jacob van Huysum (proposed spare 7); sexton of the Amstelkerk from 1718; cooperated with Picart in 1729; made the title plate for van Gool's Nieuw Schouburg (1750); a central figure of the Stadstekenacademie circle (Folkema, Morellon, Troost, Fokke, Elgersma, Tanje); works at the Westerkerk, Nieuwe Kerk and Town Hall; "exchanged brush for graver"; his collection was auctioned in 1776; Ploos ranked him with Isaac de Moucheron, de Wit and Troost. He is the reserve because he is the hinge between the painter's trade and the engraver's: the man who made the same crossing Punt made in reverse (Punt went from needle to brush late; Dubourg went from brush to graver), but his own engraved oeuvre is thin and his dates rest on one article. The doorway is the history painter who swapped the brush for the burin and taught the academy circle to etch. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#542 as a subject. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Simon Fokke (1712-1784) - the booksellers' engraver who put the whole Dutch history into small plates. Pupil of J. C. Philips (proposed spare 1); the booksellers' engraver of small portraits and vignettes; plates for Wagenaar's Vaderlandsche Historie 1749-59; Danish portraits for Tycho Hofman (1741); after Vernet, Avercamp, Terborch, Ribera and Troost (a burlesque Death of Dido); the title vignette for Rousseau's Discourse on Inequality (1755); his portrait was painted by John Greenwood after Jacobus Buys. The doorway is the booksellers' engraver who put the whole Dutch history into small plates. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#542 as a subject. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Jakob van der Schley (26 July 1715 - 7 February 1779) - the boy who finished his dead master's plates and then mapped the world for the book trade. Pupil of Picart from about age 12; completed unfinished plates after Picart's death; made the memorial portrait of Picart (1734); maps for Prevost's Histoire generale des voyages (publisher Pieter de Hondt, The Hague); illustrations for La Vie de Marianne (1735-47); Brantome frontispieces (1740); L'Imprimerie (1739); taught Philippus van der Schley (1724-1817); cooperated with Prosper Marchand. The doorway is the boy who finished his dead master's plates and then mapped the world for the book trade. Jan Caspar Philips (1690-1775; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the German engraver who taught the booksellers' engraver, and drew Sinterklaas. German engraver settled in Amsterdam; teacher of Simon Fokke (proposed alternate 1) and of his nephew Caspar Philips (proposed spare 8); plates for Wagenaar's Hedendaagse Historie; 1743 portraits of Mennonite ministers for Kornelis de Wit; Sinterklaasavond. (The 14 names carried in #540 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Johann Heinrich Roos (29 September 1631, Otterberg - 3 October 1685, Frankfurt), Thomas Heeremans (bapt. 29 May 1641, Haarlem - bur. 24 January 1694, Haarlem), Ludolf Bakhuizen (28 December 1630 or 1632, Emden - 7 November 1708, Amsterdam) (mains); Jacob Koninck (c. 1615, Amsterdam - c. 1695) (reserve); Jacob Coning (Jacob Koninck II; c. 1647, The Hague - 16 July 1724, Copenhagen), Klaes Molenaer (Nicolaes Molenaer; 1626-1629, Haarlem - 1676, Haarlem) (alternates); spares: Aernout Smit (1641, Amsterdam - 1710), Michiel Maddersteg (bapt. 5 January 1662, Enkhuizen - d. 1708, Berlin), Jan Claesz Rietschoof (21 May 1652, Hoorn - 3 November 1719, Hoorn), Ferdinand Kobell (7 June 1740, Mannheim - 1 February 1799, Munich), Franz Kobell (23 November 1749, Mannheim - 14 January 1822, Munich), Johann Georg von Dillis (26 December 1759, Gmain - 28 September 1841, Munich), Willem Romeyn (also spelled Romeijn; c. 1624, Haarlem - after 1693), Isaac de Jouderville (1612, Leiden - 1645, Amsterdam). Johann Heinrich Roos (29 September 1631, Otterberg - 3 October 1685, Frankfurt; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the German who painted Italian shepherds he never saw, and died in the fire that took them. German Baroque landscape and animal painter; family fled to Amsterdam in the Thirty Years' War (1640); trained with Guilliam du Gardijn, Cornelis de Bie and Barent Graat, but Berchem and Dujardin shaped him; worked for Ernst, Landgrave of Hesse-Rheinfels 1654-1659; court painter to Charles I Louis, Elector Palatine, 1664; moved to Frankfurt 1667; died in a house fire in 1685 trying to save his belongings. Specialised in pastoral idylls - idealized landscapes with ancient ruins, shepherds, sheep and cattle, the longing for harmony between men and animals and nature; never went to Italy, built his ruins from engravings; also one of the finest German portraitists of his time; many individual animal drawings (Albertina), intensifying the individuality of each species; Goethe called him the "Raphael of all livestock painters"; sons Philipp Peter Roos (Rosa di Tivoli, 1655-1706) and Johann Melchior Roos (1663-1731) were also painters. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the German pastoral before Kobell, the model Govert van der Leeuw painted "in the manner of" and one of the masters Kobell engraved after - the animal piece as an ideal, not a copy. The doorway is the German who painted Italian shepherds he never saw, and died in the fire that took them. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions as a study subject; no local file; not carried in #522-#539 as a subject. Thomas Heeremans (bapt. 29 May 1641, Haarlem - bur. 24 January 1694, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the winter painter who signed his name so tightly that dealers invented a man called Mans. Dutch painter and art dealer; winter landscapes, cityscapes, harbour scenes, beach views, river views, village scenes; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1664; may have been a pupil of Caesar van Everdingen (no influence of Everdingen visible - Heeremans was purely a landscape painter, not a figure painter); style shaped by Klaes Molenaer, but brighter in palette and livelier in movement, villagers skating, sledging, fishing and talking; frequently returned to Scheveningen beach and Egmond village, beach and ruins; pictures often go in pairs; collaborated with specialist figure painters - Abraham Storck painted the figures in Winter landscape with the Montelbaanstoren, Amsterdam; signed "THMANS" with the letters run together, so dealers long misread him as "Frederik Hendrik Mans". He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the staffage problem from the other side: a landscapist who hired the figures in, the mirror of van de Velde the hired staffage hand. The doorway is the winter painter who signed his name so tightly that dealers invented a man called Mans. FLAG: the page says earliest dated work 1660 and last dated work 1695, against burial 24 January 1694 - the 1695 date cannot stand as painted in his lifetime; also the 1659 "pupil of an Everdingen" record is assumed to be Caesar van Everdingen, not proven. Ludolf Bakhuizen (28 December 1630 or 1632, Emden - 7 November 1708, Amsterdam; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine painter who took the sea when the van de Veldes left. German-born Dutch painter, draughtsman, calligrapher and printmaker, the leading Dutch marine painter after both Willem van de Veldes left for England in 1672. He came to Amsterdam around 1650 as a merchant's clerk, taught himself to draw ships, studied under Allaert van Everdingen and Hendrick Dubbels, joined the painters' guild in 1663, and became famous for violent water - he went out to sea in an open boat to study storms. He painted the great View of Amsterdam and the IJ (1665) as a present for Louis XIV's minister, opened a gallery on the top floor of the Amsterdam town hall in 1699, and was visited by Cosimo III de' Medici and Peter the Great. His pupils and followers include Jan Claesz Rietschoof, Aernout Smit, Michiel Maddersteg and Abraham Storck. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is the hub of the whole marine spare set - three of this study's spares are his pupils or followers - so studying him unlocks all of them, and his open-boat storm study is a mechanism no other candidate offers. The doorway is the clerk-turned-calligrapher who rowed out into storms in an open boat to learn how water breaks, and then taught half of Amsterdam's marine painters. Jacob Koninck (c. 1615, Amsterdam - c. 1695; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the teacher whose perspective books made his pupil better than him. Dutch Golden Age landscape painter; lent his perspective books to Johannes Verkolje, who became better at perspective drawing than he was; teacher of his son Jacob II (Coning) and his younger brother Philips Koninck (carried #537); uncle of Salomon Koninck; in Dordrecht 1633-1636, Rotterdam 1637-1645, The Hague 1647-1651, Amsterdam 1658, Copenhagen by c.1676, painter to the court of Christian V of Denmark. He is the reserve because he is the hinge of the whole Koninck family and a second van de Velde disciple whose line runs to Copenhagen, but his own oeuvre is thin and his dates are the shakiest in this block. The doorway is the teacher whose perspective books made his pupil better than him. Jacob Coning (Jacob Koninck II; c. 1647, The Hague - 16 July 1724, Copenhagen; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the fellow pupil who took van de Velde's landscape to a Danish court and died in the poorhouse. Dutch-Danish painter to the Danish Court; son of Jacob Koninck; trained under his father and Adriaen van de Velde - a fellow pupil of van Bergen by the same master, and the #538 pupil list's fourth name; Copenhagen 1676, court work from 1680 (contact possibly through his wife, who worked for Queen Charlotte Amalie); one of the first painters in Denmark-Norway to specialise in topographical painting; Norway 1698-1699 for Frederiksberg Palace; with Thomas Quellinus, Hendrik Krock and others proposed an art academy in Copenhagen on 6 October 1701; also a portraitist to Danish and Norwegian nobility; despite court contact he died in the poorhouse of the Reformed Church in Store Kongensgade after repeated forced auctions. The doorway is the fellow pupil who took van de Velde's landscape to a Danish court and died in the poorhouse. Klaes Molenaer (Nicolaes Molenaer; 1626-1629, Haarlem - 1676, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Haarlem winter painter whose birth was never written down because his family was Catholic. Dutch Golden Age landscape painter and draughtsman; son of tailor Jan Mienssen Molenaer, brother of Bartholomeus and Jan Miense Molenaer (carried #535 main); Roman Catholic family, so no baptismal register and no exact birth date; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1651, dues paid yearly until 1676; winter landscape painter influenced by Jacob van Ruisdael; frozen rivers teeming with skaters, sleds, horse-drawn sleighs, a church and an inn under low winter sun; taught by the little-known Nicolaes Piemont; earliest dated work 1644; works in the Rijksmuseum, Boijmans Van Beuningen, Schwerin, Braunschweig. The doorway is the Haarlem winter painter whose birth was never written down because his family was Catholic. Further: the WahooArt/artsdot "Claes Molenaer" genre painter (born c. 1630, father Pieter Molenaer, married Maria van Hille 1654, guild 1657) contradicts the Wikipedia/RKD Nicolaes (tailor's son, guild 1651, brothers Bartholomeus and Jan Miense). These look like two Molenaers conflated under one name; do not mix them. Aernout Smit (1641, Amsterdam - 1710; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the attic painter who copied Bakhuizen on the record. Marine painter; pupil of Jan Theunisz Blanckerhoff; painted in an attic room on the Singel, probably employed by dealer Laurens Cornelisz de Coninck; follower and copyist of Ludolf Bakhuizen (1687/1689 notarial records of copying Bakhuizen); The Africa in Table Bay; most marines 1667-1678, later style tending to Bakhuizen; Amsterdam Guild of St Luke 1688; died aged about 70, buried at the Leiden cemetery. The doorway is the attic painter who copied Bakhuizen on the record. Michiel Maddersteg (bapt. 5 January 1662, Enkhuizen - d. 1708, Berlin; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine painter who built his patron real ships. Marine painter; studied under Ludolf Bakhuizen; spent most of his life at the court of Frederick I of Prussia, returned to Amsterdam later in life, where he commissioned the building of two beautiful ships for Frederick I's pleasure; most of his paintings are in Germany. The doorway is the marine painter who built his patron real ships. Jan Claesz Rietschoof (21 May 1652, Hoorn - 3 November 1719, Hoorn; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Hoorn pupil who copied his master's storm the year after it was painted. Dutch Golden Age seascape painter; pupil of Abraham Liedts, then of Ludolf Bakhuizen; active in Hoorn all his career, possibly some time in Amsterdam with Bakhuizen; taught his son Hendrik; one of Bakhuizen's most accomplished pupils (Sotheby's, on a stormy sea after Bakhuizen's Fishing Vessels Offshore in a Heavy Sea, 1684, completed 1685); works in the Rijksmuseum, Het Scheepvaartmuseum, Westfries Museum Hoorn, the Hermitage, the National Maritime Museum Greenwich; Hoorn contemporary of the flower painter Jacob Rootius, son of Jan Albertsz Rotius. The doorway is the Hoorn pupil who copied his master's storm the year after it was painted. Ferdinand Kobell (7 June 1740, Mannheim - 1 February 1799, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the law student who became the etcher of the Dutch landscape in Germany. German painter and copper engraver; grandson of Johann Heinrich Kobell, grocer of Frankfurt who settled in Mannheim in 1720; studied law at Heidelberg, then painting under Peter Verschatfelt; court painter 1766; Paris 1768-1769 under Johann Georg Wille; followed Elector Charles Theodore to Munich 1794; named Director of the Palatinate Painting Gallery 1798 but died before taking up the post; landscapes in the style of Berchem; about 300 etchings, published as Oeuvres completes (1809), marking a distinct advance in German landscape etching; father of Wilhelm, brother of Franz. The doorway is the law student who became the etcher of the Dutch landscape in Germany. Franz Kobell (23 November 1749, Mannheim - 14 January 1822, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the uncle who drew twenty thousand landscapes because paint was too slow. German painter, etcher and draftsman; brother of Ferdinand, uncle and teacher of Wilhelm; sent to Italy by Elector Karl Theodor 1776, in Rome until 1785, working from nature and monumental buildings; court painter at Munich; produced only a few oil paintings, highly praised by Goethe (Rocky Landscape with Waterfalls, once in the Bamberg gallery), but left 20,000 landscape and architecture pen drawings and etchings, painting being too slow to keep pace with his ideas. The doorway is the uncle who drew twenty thousand landscapes because paint was too slow. Johann Georg von Dillis (26 December 1759, Gmain - 28 September 1841, Munich; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the professor Kobell replaced, who taught Munich to paint what it saw. German landscape painter, pioneer of Naturalism; trained under Johann Jakob Dorner the elder and at the Munich Zeichnungsakademie; inspector of the Hofgarten Galerie 1790; professor of landscape painting at the Munich Academy 1808-1814, replaced by Wilhelm von Kobell in 1813/1814; central gallery director 1822, founding director of what became the Alte Pinakothek; mentor of Carl Rottmann and Ludwig Richter; insisted on Naturstudium, direct study of nature, against the history-painting hierarchy. The doorway is the professor Kobell replaced, who taught Munich to paint what it saw. Willem Romeyn (also spelled Romeijn; c. 1624, Haarlem - after 1693; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the Berchem pupil whose pastures Kobell was still copying two centuries later. Dutch Italianate landscape painter and printmaker; pupil of Nicolaes Berchem around 1642; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1646; sun-drenched southern landscapes with shepherds, cattle, sheep and goats resting by rivers, long shadows, hazy golden light; last dated works 1694; Kobell etched after him (The Shepherd's Rest, 1819, after Romeyn). The doorway is the Berchem pupil whose pastures Kobell was still copying two centuries later. Isaac de Jouderville (1612, Leiden - 1645, Amsterdam; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the orphan pupil whose tronies were filed under Rembrandt for centuries. Dutch Golden Age painter, pupil of Rembrandt; orphan, parents from Metz; apprenticed to Rembrandt from 1 November 1629, six signed receipts for 1630-1631, travelled with him to Amsterdam in 1631; enrolled at Leiden University April 1632; married Maria le Febure 1636; Deventer 1641; back in Amsterdam 1643, died young in 1645; his widow married glassmaker Pieter de Melder in 1648, and his daughter Mariecke later married Frederik de Moucheron - the Jouderville line marries straight into the Moucheron line; painted mainly Rembrandtesque heads or tronies, so faithful that several of his paintings were previously attributed to Rembrandt; Man in Oriental Costume was the Fake or Fortune? case (BBC, 2011), a Nazi-seized Oppenheimer stock picture. The doorway is the orphan pupil whose tronies were filed under Rembrandt for centuries. Extra verified spares (not part of the 8 above, all verified NOT STUDIED): Jan Baptist Boel the Younger (between September and 4 October 1643, Antwerp - 9 January 1689; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S22). Son and pupil of Pieter Boel; family moved to Paris 1668 for the Gobelins; married Maria Catharina Immenraet, daughter of landscape painter Philips Augustijn Immenraet, 4 May 1675; collaborated with other Antwerp painters, adding the still-life and animal elements to their compositions; known oeuvre very limited; assimilated his father's style so completely that attribution problems follow (Nationalmuseum). Held back because his oeuvre is small and mostly indistinguishable from his father's. VERIFIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#539. Jan Theunisz Blanckerhoff (also Blankerhoff/Blankhof/Jan Maat; 10 January 1628, Alkmaar - bur. 2 October 1669, Amsterdam; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S26). Marine painter; taught by Arent Teerling, Pieter Scheyenburg and Caesar van Everdingen; served on a Dutch warship; Rome, Bentvueghels, called Jan Maat; Italian ports with strange boats his most popular pieces; teacher of Aernout Smit; Battle of the Zuiderzee 1663-1668, 700 guilders, Rijksmuseum SK-A-3235. Held back because his career is short (work period 1649-1669) and his record is thin. Hendrik Kobell (13 September 1751, Rotterdam - 3 August 1779, Rotterdam; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S29). Dutch landscape and marine painter, etcher, draftsman, watercolourist; cousin of Ferdinand and Franz Kobell; father trained him in drawing, he loved drawing ships in Rotterdam port; London 1769-1770, Paris 1772, Amsterdam Stadstekenakademie; founder of the Rotterdam drawing society Hierdoor tot Hooger; father and teacher of Jan Kobell II (1778-1814); pupils Carel Frederik Bendorp and Gerrit van der Pals; The Shipwreck (1775, Rijksmuseum SK-A-1218). New names from Kobell's own network -- verified NOT STUDIED, for a later round (NOT part of the 8 extra spares above): Carl Rottmann (11 January 1797, Handschuhsheim - 7 July 1850, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source). German landscape painter, most famous of the Rottmann family. First drawing lessons from his father Friedrich, who taught drawing at Heidelberg University; moved to Munich in 1821/1822, married Friederike von Sckell in 1824, and through her came to the attention of Crown Prince Ludwig, who sent him to Italy in 1826/1827 to sketch the 28 Italian landscapes in fresco for the Hofgarten arcades (completed 1833), then commissioned a Greek cycle in 1834 (23 large landscapes, now in the Neue Pinakothek). Court painter in 1841. His school included Karl Lindemann-Frommel, Karl Ludwig Seeger, August Loffler and August Seidel. WHY: the Munich landscapist who raised landscape to the height of history painting in fresco - the direct successor to Dillis's and Kobell's Munich. The doorway is: from Dillis's Munich, hop to the painter Ludwig sent to Italy to fresco a whole arcade of landscapes. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file (parent verified both). Peter von Cornelius (23 September 1783, Dusseldorf - 6 March 1867, Berlin; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source). German painter, a main representative of the Nazarene movement. Drawing classes at the Dusseldorf Academy from age twelve; Rome 1811-1819 with the Lukasbund; summoned to Munich by Crown Prince Ludwig in 1819 to decorate the Glyptothek; director of the Dusseldorf Academy in 1821 and of the Munich Academy from 1824/1825; his "Last Judgment" (1829-1840) fills the east wall of the Ludwigskirche in Munich, 19 m by 12 m. His pupils included Karl Hermann, Wilhelm von Kaulbach and Adam Eberle; he left Munich for Berlin in 1839-1840. WHY: the academy director who rebuilt German painting around fresco and the Munich school - the institutional successor to Kobell's generation. The doorway is: from Kobell's Munich Academy, hop to the Nazarene who took its directorship and covered a church wall with the Last Judgment. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file (the file cornelius-cardew.md in the studies directory is Cornelius Cardew, a different person; parent verified both).



(The 14 names carried in #539 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Jacob de Wit (bapt. 19 December 1695, Amsterdam - d. 12 November 1754, Amsterdam), Johann Heinrich Roos (29 September 1631, Otterberg - 3 October 1685, Frankfurt), Thomas Heeremans (bapt. 29 May 1641, Haarlem - bur. 24 January 1694, Haarlem) (mains); Jacob Koninck (c. 1615, Amsterdam - c. 1695) (reserve); Jacob Coning (Jacob Koninck II; c. 1647, The Hague - 16 July 1724, Copenhagen), Klaes Molenaer (Nicolaes Molenaer; 1626-1629, Haarlem - 1676, Haarlem) (alternates); spares: Aernout Smit (1641, Amsterdam - 1710), Michiel Maddersteg (bapt. 5 January 1662, Enkhuizen - d. 1708, Berlin), Jan Claesz Rietschoof (21 May 1652, Hoorn - 3 November 1719, Hoorn), Ferdinand Kobell (7 June 1740, Mannheim - 1 February 1799, Munich), Franz Kobell (23 November 1749, Mannheim - 14 January 1822, Munich), Johann Georg von Dillis (26 December 1759, Gmain - 28 September 1841, Munich), Willem Romeyn (also spelled Romeijn; c. 1624, Haarlem - 1694, Haarlem), Isaac de Jouderville (1612, Leiden - 1645, Amsterdam). Jacob de Wit (bapt. 19 December 1695, Amsterdam - d. 12 November 1754, Amsterdam; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the man who painted stone that was not there, on the ceilings of Amsterdam. Dutch Rococo painter and interior decorator; pupil of Albert van Spiers and Jacob van Hal, Antwerp Guild of St Luke 1714; copied Rubens's ceilings in the Carolus Borromeuskerk in watercolour 1711-1722, the record after lightning destroyed them in 1718 (engraved by his pupil Jan Punt, 1751); famous for door and ceiling paintings in Amsterdam canal houses (he lived on the Keizersgracht) and country houses on the Vecht; grisailles he called "witjes" - grey-white paintings faking stone relief; Moses Choosing the Seventy Elders (1737, Amsterdam Town Hall), Apotheosis of Aeneas for Pieter Pels (Herengracht 479), ceiling with Isaac de Moucheron; pupils Jan Punt, Pieter Tanje, Dionys van Nijmegen, Jan de Groot, Frans and Jacob Xavery; follower Tako Hajo Jelgersma. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the Moucheron line's other half - the figure painter inside the wall-painter's room - and the witje is the one trompe-l'oeil mechanism this chain has never studied. The doorway is the man who painted stone that was not there, on the ceilings of Amsterdam. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. FLAG: one conservation page heads him "(1685-1754)" while its own body gives baptized 19 December 1695 - the 1685 is a typo. Johann Heinrich Roos (29 September 1631, Otterberg - 3 October 1685, Frankfurt; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the German who painted Italian shepherds he never saw, and died in the fire that took them. German Baroque landscape and animal painter; family fled to Amsterdam in the Thirty Years' War (1640); trained with Guilliam du Gardijn, Cornelis de Bie and Barent Graat, but Berchem and Dujardin shaped him; worked for Ernst, Landgrave of Hesse-Rheinfels 1654-1659; court painter to Charles I Louis, Elector Palatine, 1664; moved to Frankfurt 1667; died in a house fire in 1685 trying to save his belongings. Specialised in pastoral idylls - idealized landscapes with ancient ruins, shepherds, sheep and cattle, the longing for harmony between men and animals and nature; never went to Italy, built his ruins from engravings; also one of the finest German portraitists of his time; many individual animal drawings (Albertina), intensifying the individuality of each species; Goethe called him the "Raphael of all livestock painters"; sons Philipp Peter Roos (Rosa di Tivoli, 1655-1706) and Johann Melchior Roos (1663-1731) were also painters. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the German pastoral before Kobell, the model Govert van der Leeuw painted "in the manner of" and one of the masters Kobell engraved after - the animal piece as an ideal, not a copy. The doorway is the German who painted Italian shepherds he never saw, and died in the fire that took them. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Thomas Heeremans (bapt. 29 May 1641, Haarlem - bur. 24 January 1694, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the winter painter who signed his name so tightly that dealers invented a man called Mans. Dutch painter and art dealer; winter landscapes, cityscapes, harbour scenes, beach views, river views, village scenes; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1664; may have been a pupil of Caesar van Everdingen (no influence of Everdingen visible - Heeremans was purely a landscape painter, not a figure painter); style shaped by Klaes Molenaer, but brighter in palette and livelier in movement, villagers skating, sledging, fishing and talking; frequently returned to Scheveningen beach and Egmond village, beach and ruins; pictures often go in pairs; collaborated with specialist figure painters - Abraham Storck painted the figures in Winter landscape with the Montelbaanstoren, Amsterdam; signed "THMANS" with the letters run together, so dealers long misread him as "Frederik Hendrik Mans". He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is the staffage problem from the other side: a landscapist who hired the figures in, the mirror of van de Velde the hired staffage hand. The doorway is the winter painter who signed his name so tightly that dealers invented a man called Mans. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. FLAG: the page says earliest dated work 1660 and last dated work 1695, against burial 24 January 1694 - the 1695 date cannot stand as painted in his lifetime; also the 1659 "pupil of an Everdingen" record is assumed to be Caesar van Everdingen, not proven. Jacob Koninck (c. 1615, Amsterdam - c. 1695; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the teacher whose perspective books made his pupil better than him. Dutch Golden Age landscape painter; lent his perspective books to Johannes Verkolje, who became better at perspective drawing than he was; teacher of his son Jacob II (Coning) and his younger brother Philips Koninck (carried #537); uncle of Salomon Koninck; in Dordrecht 1633-1636, Rotterdam 1637-1645, The Hague 1647-1651, Amsterdam 1658, Copenhagen by c.1676, painter to the court of Christian V of Denmark. He is the reserve because he is the hinge of the whole Koninck family and a second van de Velde disciple whose line runs to Copenhagen, but his own oeuvre is thin and his dates are the shakiest in this block. The doorway is the teacher whose perspective books made his pupil better than him. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. FLAG: teacher conflict - Houbraken says disciple of Adriaen van de Velde, RKD says pupil of David Colijns; death c.1695 (Wikipedia) vs 1666 (artsdot, wrong) vs 1690 or later (British Museum); birth c.1612 (Sphinx) vs c.1615 (Wikipedia/British Museum 1614/15). Jacob Coning (Jacob Koninck II; c. 1647, The Hague - 16 July 1724, Copenhagen; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the fellow pupil who took van de Velde's landscape to a Danish court and died in the poorhouse. Dutch-Danish painter to the Danish Court; son of Jacob Koninck; trained under his father and Adriaen van de Velde - a fellow pupil of van Bergen by the same master, and the #538 pupil list's fourth name; Copenhagen 1676, court work from 1680 (contact possibly through his wife, who worked for Queen Charlotte Amalie); one of the first painters in Denmark-Norway to specialise in topographical painting; Norway 1698-1699 for Frederiksberg Palace; with Thomas Quellinus, Hendrik Krock and others proposed an art academy in Copenhagen on 6 October 1701; also a portraitist to Danish and Norwegian nobility; despite court contact he died in the poorhouse of the Reformed Church in Store Kongensgade after repeated forced auctions. The doorway is the fellow pupil who took van de Velde's landscape to a Danish court and died in the poorhouse. Klaes Molenaer (Nicolaes Molenaer; 1626-1629, Haarlem - 1676, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Haarlem winter painter whose birth was never written down because his family was Catholic. Dutch Golden Age landscape painter and draughtsman; son of tailor Jan Mienssen Molenaer, brother of Bartholomeus and Jan Miense Molenaer (carried #535 main); Roman Catholic family, so no baptismal register and no exact birth date; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1651, dues paid yearly until 1676; winter landscape painter influenced by Jacob van Ruisdael; frozen rivers teeming with skaters, sleds, horse-drawn sleighs, a church and an inn under low winter sun; taught by the little-known Nicolaes Piemont; earliest dated work 1644; works in the Rijksmuseum, Boijmans Van Beuningen, Schwerin, Braunschweig. The doorway is the Haarlem winter painter whose birth was never written down because his family was Catholic. Aernout Smit (1641, Amsterdam - 1710; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the attic painter who copied Bakhuizen on the record. Marine painter; pupil of Jan Theunisz Blanckerhoff; painted in an attic room on the Singel, probably employed by dealer Laurens Cornelisz de Coninck; follower and copyist of Ludolf Bakhuizen (1687/1689 notarial records of copying Bakhuizen); The Africa in Table Bay; most marines 1667-1678, later style tending to Bakhuizen; Amsterdam Guild of St Luke 1688; died aged about 70, buried at the Leiden cemetery. The doorway is the attic painter who copied Bakhuizen on the record. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Dates search-verified only, from GAC, John Bennett, Bijl-Van-Urk - no EN Wikipedia article exists. FLAG: birth 1640/41 per a 1667 document giving his age as "about 26"; John Bennett gives Bakhuizen as "1631-1701", which is wrong (1708). Michiel Maddersteg (bapt. 5 January 1662, Enkhuizen - d. 1708, Berlin; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine painter who built his patron real ships. Marine painter; studied under Ludolf Bakhuizen; spent most of his life at the court of Frederick I of Prussia, returned to Amsterdam later in life, where he commissioned the building of two beautiful ships for Frederick I's pleasure; most of his paintings are in Germany. The doorway is the marine painter who built his patron real ships. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. FLAG: an earlier working assumption of death 1743 is WRONG; Wikipedia and Houbraken give died Berlin 1708. Jan Claesz Rietschoof (21 May 1652, Hoorn - 3 November 1719, Hoorn; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Hoorn pupil who copied his master's storm the year after it was painted. Dutch Golden Age seascape painter; pupil of Abraham Liedts, then of Ludolf Bakhuizen; active in Hoorn all his career, possibly some time in Amsterdam with Bakhuizen; taught his son Hendrik; one of Bakhuizen's most accomplished pupils (Sotheby's, on a stormy sea after Bakhuizen's Fishing Vessels Offshore in a Heavy Sea, 1684, completed 1685); works in the Rijksmuseum, Het Scheepvaartmuseum, Westfries Museum Hoorn, the Hermitage, the National Maritime Museum Greenwich; Hoorn contemporary of the flower painter Jacob Rootius, son of Jan Albertsz Rotius. The doorway is the Hoorn pupil who copied his master's storm the year after it was painted. Ferdinand Kobell (7 June 1740, Mannheim - 1 February 1799, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the law student who became the etcher of the Dutch landscape in Germany. German painter and copper engraver; grandson of Johann Heinrich Kobell, grocer of Frankfurt who settled in Mannheim in 1720; studied law at Heidelberg, then painting under Peter Verschatfelt; court painter 1766; Paris 1768-1769 under Johann Georg Wille; followed Elector Charles Theodore to Munich 1794; named Director of the Palatinate Painting Gallery 1798 but died before taking up the post; landscapes in the style of Berchem; about 300 etchings, published as Oeuvres completes (1809), marking a distinct advance in German landscape etching; father of Wilhelm, brother of Franz. The doorway is the law student who became the etcher of the Dutch landscape in Germany. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Franz Kobell (23 November 1749, Mannheim - 14 January 1822, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the uncle who drew twenty thousand landscapes because paint was too slow. German painter, etcher and draftsman; brother of Ferdinand, uncle and teacher of Wilhelm; sent to Italy by Elector Karl Theodor 1776, in Rome until 1785, working from nature and monumental buildings; court painter at Munich; produced only a few oil paintings, highly praised by Goethe (Rocky Landscape with Waterfalls, once in the Bamberg gallery), but left 20,000 landscape and architecture pen drawings and etchings, painting being too slow to keep pace with his ideas. The doorway is the uncle who drew twenty thousand landscapes because paint was too slow. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Johann Georg von Dillis (26 December 1759, Gmain - 28 September 1841, Munich; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the professor Kobell replaced, who taught Munich to paint what it saw. German landscape painter, pioneer of Naturalism; trained under Johann Jakob Dorner the elder and at the Munich Zeichnungsakademie; inspector of the Hofgarten Galerie 1790; professor of landscape painting at the Munich Academy 1808-1814, replaced by Wilhelm von Kobell in 1813/1814; central gallery director 1822, founding director of what became the Alte Pinakothek; mentor of Carl Rottmann and Ludwig Richter; insisted on Naturstudium, direct study of nature, against the history-painting hierarchy. The doorway is the professor Kobell replaced, who taught Munich to paint what it saw. Willem Romeyn (also spelled Romeijn; c. 1624, Haarlem - 1694, Haarlem; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the Berchem pupil whose pastures Kobell was still copying two centuries later. Dutch Italianate landscape painter and printmaker; pupil of Nicolaes Berchem around 1642; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1646; sun-drenched southern landscapes with shepherds, cattle, sheep and goats resting by rivers, long shadows, hazy golden light; last dated works 1694; Kobell etched after him (The Shepherd's Rest, 1819, after Romeyn). The doorway is the Berchem pupil whose pastures Kobell was still copying two centuries later. Isaac de Jouderville (1612, Leiden - 1645, Amsterdam; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the orphan pupil whose tronies were filed under Rembrandt for centuries. Dutch Golden Age painter, pupil of Rembrandt; orphan, parents from Metz; apprenticed to Rembrandt from 1 November 1629, six signed receipts for 1630-1631, travelled with him to Amsterdam in 1631; enrolled at Leiden University April 1632; married Maria le Febure 1636; Deventer 1641; back in Amsterdam 1643, died young in 1645; his widow married glassmaker Pieter de Melder in 1648, and his daughter Mariecke later married Frederik de Moucheron - the Jouderville line marries straight into the Moucheron line; painted mainly Rembrandtesque heads or tronies, so faithful that several of his paintings were previously attributed to Rembrandt; Man in Oriental Costume was the Fake or Fortune? case (BBC, 2011), a Nazi-seized Oppenheimer stock picture. The doorway is the orphan pupil whose tronies were filed under Rembrandt for centuries. Extra verified spares (not part of the 8 above, all verified NOT STUDIED): Jan Baptist Boel the Younger (between September and 4 October 1643, Antwerp - 9 January 1689; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S22). Son and pupil of Pieter Boel; family moved to Paris 1668 for the Gobelins; married Maria Catharina Immenraet, daughter of landscape painter Philips Augustijn Immenraet, 4 May 1675; collaborated with other Antwerp painters, adding the still-life and animal elements to their compositions; known oeuvre very limited; assimilated his father's style so completely that attribution problems follow (Nationalmuseum). Held back because his oeuvre is small and mostly indistinguishable from his father's. VERIFIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Jan Theunisz Blanckerhoff (also Blankerhoff/Blankhof/Jan Maat; 10 January 1628, Alkmaar - bur. 2 October 1669, Amsterdam; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S26). Marine painter; taught by Arent Teerling, Pieter Scheyenburg and Caesar van Everdingen; served on a Dutch warship; Rome, Bentvueghels, called Jan Maat; Italian ports with strange boats his most popular pieces; teacher of Aernout Smit; Battle of the Zuiderzee 1663-1668, 700 guilders, Rijksmuseum SK-A-3235. Held back because his career is short (work period 1649-1669) and his record is thin. Nephew of Philips Koninck; apprenticed to his uncle Jacob Koninck at the Copenhagen court 1687-1690; moved to England 1690 (Oxford, then London by 1720); genre works and portraits/tronies in the manner of Rembrandt; Peter King, 1st Baron King of Ockham (1720, National Portrait Gallery). Held back because he is a portraitist, off the landscape line. VERIFIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Hendrik Kobell (13 September 1751, Rotterdam - 3 August 1779, Rotterdam; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S29). Dutch landscape and marine painter, etcher, draftsman, watercolourist; cousin of Ferdinand and Franz Kobell; father trained him in drawing, he loved drawing ships in Rotterdam port; London 1769-1770, Paris 1772, Amsterdam Stadstekenakademie; founder of the Rotterdam drawing society Hierdoor tot Hooger; father and teacher of Jan Kobell II (1778-1814); pupils Carel Frederik Bendorp and Gerrit van der Pals; The Shipwreck (1775, Rijksmuseum SK-A-1218). Held back because he died at 27 and the record is short. VERIFIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Ludolf Bakhuizen (28 December 1630 - 7 November 1708; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine painter who took the sea when the van de Veldes left: clerk and calligrapher turned painter, student of Everdingen then Dubbels, who went out in an open boat to study storms and became the leading Dutch marine painter after 1672. He is main 2 rather than top pick because the hop runs through the pupil first, and Bakhuizen is the sea line, not the cattle line. The doorway is the clerk who went out in an open boat to study storms. Death 7 November 1708 is consistent across sources. Pieter Boel (bapt. 10 October 1622, Antwerp - d. 3 September 1674, Paris; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the live-animal man: pupil of Jan Fyt, worked from live animals at the Versailles menagerie, peintre ordinaire to Louis XIV in 1674; the hunt line carried from Antwerp to the French court. He is main 3 rather than top pick because the hop runs through the pupil and the marine line, not through the menagerie. The doorway is the man who painted from live animals at Versailles. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is Boel's fellow pupil of the menagerie line, and the study should meet Boel first. The doorway is the Snyders pupil who drew 52 species for the king. Reserve: Jan van de Cappelle (bapt. 25 January 1626 - bur. 22 December 1679; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the calm-water mirror: dyeworks heir who never needed to sell a picture, painted fewer than 150 works in which dead-calm water mirrors the clouds; collector of 192 paintings and over 7,000 drawings, the only man painted by both Rembrandt and Frans Hals; the man who taught Willem the Younger how to paint a sky reflected in water. He is the reserve because his mechanism set (the mirror water, the collector as painter) is the most specialised in the network, a strong contrast study whenever the chain wants one. The doorway is the dyeworks heir whose calm water mirrors the clouds. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#537. (The 14 names carried in #537 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Jan Weenix (1642-1719), Frans Snyders (11 November 1579 - 19 August 1657), Paul de Vos (1591/92 or 1595 - 30 June 1678) (mains); Melchior d'Hondecoeter (c. 1636 - 3 April 1695) (reserve); Jan Hackaert (bapt. February 1628 - in/after 1685), Willem van de Velde the Younger (bapt. 18 December 1633 - 6 April 1707) (alternates); spares: Gerrit Berckheyde (bapt. 6 June 1638 - 10 June 1698), Pieter de Molijn (bapt. 6 April 1595 - 23 March 1661), Caesar van Everdingen (1616/17 - bur. 13 October 1678), Pieter de Grebber (c. 1600 - 1652/53), Willem Cornelisz Duyster (1599-1635), Salomon van Ruysdael (c. 1602 - bur. 3 November 1670), Willem van de Velde the Elder (c. 1611 - 13 December 1693), Jan Fyt (bapt. 15 March 1611 - 11 September 1661). Jan Weenix (1642-1719; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the hunt line carried into dead-game still life. Trained by his father Jan Baptist Weenix (carried #531) with his cousin Melchior d'Hondecoeter; dead game and hunting scenes; Utrecht guild 1664 and 1668; painted Peter the Great 1697; court painter to Elector Palatine Johann Wilhelm 1702-1712/14, 12 large hunting pictures for Schloss Bensberg; pupils his daughter Maria Weenix and Dirk Valkenburg (carried #536 extra spare); Goethe devoted a poem to his animal textures; the Wallace Collection holds 13 paintings. He is main 2 rather than top pick because the copy line runs through the cattle painter first, and Weenix is the still-life end of the hunt, not its living middle. The doorway is the hunter who became the still-life painter of dead game. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#536. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. FLAG: birth year uncertain - sources give between 1641 and 1649; 1642 is the commonly cited year; the page notes he married in 1679 "around thirty" and had 13 children 1680-1700. Frans Snyders (11 November 1579 - 19 August 1657; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Antwerp root of the whole hunt and animal line. Pupil of Pieter Brueghel the Younger 1593, then Hendrick van Balen; master 1602; Italy 1608-09 (Rome, Milan, Cardinal Borromeo); married Margaretha de Vos 1611 (sister of Cornelis and Paul de Vos); Rubens collaboration from the 1610s; Torre de la Parada 1636-38, about 60 hunting and animal pieces after Rubens designs, plus 18 more 1639; dean 1628; pupils Nicasius Bernaerts, Peter van Boucle, Juriaen Jacobsze, Jan Roos, Paul de Vos; Jan Fyt student and assistant from 1629; patrons Archduke Albert VII, Philip IV of Spain, Bishop Antonius Triest; influenced Jan Davidsz. de Heem and the 18th-century French animaliers Oudry and Desportes; the concert of birds theme; died childless. He is main 3 rather than top pick because the hop runs through the son's copying, not through Snyders directly, and his scale would swamp the Haarlem chain. The doorway is the Antwerp root of the hunt. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#536. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Paul de Vos (1591/92 or 1595 - 30 June 1678; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Snyders's brother-in-law and workshop colleague; the second Antwerp hunt painter. Pupil of Denijs van Hove 1604, David Remeeus 1605, Eduard Snayers; worked in his brother-in-law Frans Snyders' workshop (Snyders married his sister Margaretha 1611); guild master 1620; monumental hunts for aristocrats, warmer colours and broader brush than Snyders, fighting cats, horses attacked by wolves; collaborator with Rubens (Torre de la Parada), Van Dyck, Jan Wildens; patrons Marquis of Leganes and Philippe-Charles 3rd Count of Arenberg (36+ paintings 1633-1640); many works long misattributed to Snyders; never dated his paintings. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is Snyders's shadow, and the study should meet Snyders first. The doorway is the brother-in-law in Snyders's shadow who painted the fighting cats. Reserve: Melchior d'Hondecoeter (c. 1636 - 3 April 1695; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the bird painter: grandson of Gillis, son of Gijsbert, nephew of J. B. Weenix, cousin of Jan Weenix; trained by his father Gijsbert then his uncle J. B. Weenix; Confrerie Pictura 1659; married Susanne Tradel 1663; painted almost exclusively birds (exotic and game) in park landscapes; about 250 works, only about 20 dated; pupils Willem Hendrik Wilhelmus van Royen and Jan Weenix; employed by William III to paint his menagerie at Het Loo; his inventory lists a small gallows (to pose birds) and seven Snyders paintings; followers include Adriaen Coorte, Jan van Huysum, Elias Vonck; early sea-pieces and Tub with Fish 1655 before switching to birds. He is the reserve because his mechanism set (the bird as still life in a park landscape, the menagerie as subject) is the most specialised in the network, a strong contrast study whenever the chain wants one. The doorway is the bird painter with a gallows in his inventory. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#536. Germany and Switzerland, Lake of Zurich c. 1660-64 (long mistaken for an Italian lake); influenced by Jan Both (studied #533) and Asselijn (carried #532); staffage by Berchem, Adriaen van de Velde, Lingelbach; painted landscape backgrounds for Berchem and van de Velde; owned and copied Jan Both works; latest signed work 1685. The doorway is the forest painter whose Swiss lake was mistaken for Italy. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#536. Willem van de Velde the Younger (bapt. 18 December 1633 - 6 April 1707; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine line: son of Willem the Elder, elder brother of Adriaen; trained by his father then Simon de Vlieger (Weesp); Amsterdam until 1672, then England with his father, Charles II service, studio at Queen's House Greenwich, Westminster 1691; leading Dutch marine painter of the later 17th century. He is an alternate rather than a main because the marine line is a step sideways from the hunt, and his brother is the top pick. The doorway is the elder brother who painted the sea while the younger painted the cattle. His is the study of the Haarlem townscape painter who also painted cavalry pieces, the town answer to Oudendijck's camp. The doorway is the Haarlem townscape painter the son followed. Pieter de Molijn (bapt. 6 April 1595 London - 23 March 1661 Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Guild Haarlem 1616, dean 1633, 1638, 1646; tonal-phase pioneer with Jan van Goyen and Salomon van Ruysdael; pupils Gerard ter Borch the Younger and Elder, Allaert van Everdingen, Jan Wils, Jan Coelenbier; collaborated with ter Borch, Frans Hals, Pieter de Grebber, Jacob de Wet, Jacob Pynas, Nicolaes Berchem; son Anthony de Molijn (1635-c. 1703). His is the study of the tonal pioneer who taught the generation before Oudendijck. The doorway is the tonal pioneer who taught ter Borch and Everdingen. Willem van de Velde the Elder (c. 1611 - 13 December 1693; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - pen paintings (penschilderij) in black ink on vellum, panel and canvas; sailed with the fleet sketching battles (Scheveningen 1653, Sole Bay 1672, about 70 sketches); Charles II paid him 100 pounds a year for "draughts of sea fights" and the same to his son to colour them; father of Willem the Younger and Adriaen, and daughter Magdalena (b. 1632); Amsterdam studio on Nieuwe Waalseiland; commissions from Admiral Cornelis Tromp; died London 1693, buried St James's Piccadilly. His is the study of the father who drew sea battles in pen while his sons painted them in oil. The doorway is the father who sketched the fleet in pen. Jan Fyt (bapt. 15 March 1611 - 11 September 1661; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Snyders's pupil and assistant 1629-31, master 1629/30; Italy 1633-41 (Paris, Venice for the Sagredo and Contarini families, Rome 1635, Bentvueghels bent-name "Goudvink", bullfinch); back in Antwerp by 1641; Guild of Romanists dean 1652; married Francoise van de Sande 22 March 1654, four children; about 380 paintings (287 extant still lifes per Christie's); moved the dead-game display from the indoor table to the open landscape, the first to paint game as a trophy of the hunt rather than food; pupils Pieter Boel and Jacob van der Kerckhoven; collaborators Cornelius Schut, Thomas Willeboirts Bosschaert, Erasmus Quellinus II; Set of the Dogs 1642 dedicated to Don Carlo Guasco, Marquess of Soleno. His is the study of the man who took dead game out of the kitchen and into the landscape. The doorway is the pupil who moved the game from the table to the field. Jan Porcellis (1580/84 Ghent - 29 January 1632 Zoeterwoude; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - marine painter; initiated the decisive transition from early realism to the tonal phase: overcast skies, rough waters, low horizons, two-thirds of the canvas sea and sky; supposed pupil of Hendrick Vroom (per Houbraken); Antwerp guild 1617, Rotterdam by 1615, Haarlem around 1622; teacher of Simon de Vlieger per the Royal Museums Greenwich; the most influential marine painter of the first half of the 17th century; only about ten works securely identified. Hercules Segers (c. 1589 - c. 1638; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Haarlem-born; pupil of Gillis van Coninxloo; the most inspired, experimental and original landscapist of his period and an even more innovative printmaker; 54 plates, 183 known impressions, about 15 paintings; printed in coloured ink on coloured paper and cloth, hand-coloured, cropped to different sizes, each impression a miniature painting; invented lift-ground (sugar-bite) etching, not used again for 150 years; Rembrandt owned eight of his paintings and a printing plate; art dealer by trade; wife recorded as a widow in 1638. (The 14 names carried in #536 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Salomon de Bray (1597 - 11 May 1664), Leendert van der Cooghen (9 May 1632 - 18 February 1681), Dirk Maas (12 September 1659 - 25 December 1717) (mains); Jan van Huchtenburg (bapt. 20 November 1647 - bur. 2 July 1733) (reserve); Jan Wyck (29 October 1645 - 17 May 1702), Jacob Gole (1660-1737) (alternates); spares: Egbert van Heemskerck the Elder (1634 - c. 1702/04), Evert Collier (26 January 1642 - few days before 8 September 1708), Adriaen Oudendijck (1677-1704), Jacob Jordaens (19 May 1593 - 18 October 1678), Rachel Ruysch (3 June 1664 - 12 October 1750), Jan van Gool (1685-1763), Gerrit Claesz Bleker (1592 - 8 February 1656), John Wootton (c. 1682 - 13 November 1764). Salomon de Bray (1597 - 11 May 1664; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the draftsman whose figure studies Dusart kept: they sit in Dusart's 1704 probate inventory alongside Bega's 125 figure studies, so Dusart the collector valued de Bray's drawing as highly as his own master's circle. He is also the Haarlem classicist against whom the whole peasant line defines itself: history painter, architect of the City Hall and the Zijlpoort, town planner, poet, designer of silverwork, author of Architectura Moderna (1631), headman of the Guild of St. Luke who tried and failed to rewrite its charter in 1631 to put painting above the crafts. He died of plague in 1664 with four of his children; his sons Jan, Dirck and Joseph carried the name on, Jan already carried as a #535 spare. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he predates Dusart by a generation and the hop runs through the probate inventory, not through a shared master. The doorway is the classicist whose figure studies Dusart kept in his own inventory. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#535. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Leendert van der Cooghen (9 May 1632 - 18 February 1681; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the other draftsman in Dusart's probate inventory, and the closest parallel to Dusart himself as a draughtsman: an amateur of independent means who never had to sell, about 66 drawings and only 3 certain paintings, figure and portrait studies in red and black chalk drawn directly from the life model, dated day-month-year on 46 sheets so a chronology can be built, moving from a static dry style to a looser freer one. He trained with Jacob Jordaens, shared a studio with Cornelis Bega, befriended Vincent van der Vinne, and never married - "uncle Leendert" of the Schagelstraat, the bachelor counterpart to Dusart the bachelor who lived with his aunts. His mechanism set (the dated single-figure chalk study, the amateur who draws only when he feels like it) is the direct mirror of Dusart's own single-peasant chalks. The doorway is the amateur whose dated chalk studies Dusart collected and whose life mirrors his own. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#535. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Dirk Maas (12 September 1659 - 25 December 1717; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the pupil-chain hop with a direct Dusart document behind it: he was back in Haarlem in May 1692 on the testimony of Cornelis Dusart himself (Bredius), and he is the painter who took the Haarlem line to war - pupil of Mommers, then Berchem, then Jan van Huchtenburg, then painter of the Battle of the Boyne first-hand in 1690 for William III and for his patron Hans Willem Bentinck, Earl of Portland. He also did staffage for other painters and worked in the same studio as Adriaen Oudendijck 1695-1700, so he ties the Oudendijck top pick to the Huchtenburg reserve and the Wyck alternate in one knot. The doorway is the horse painter Dusart himself testified for, who painted the Boyne first-hand. FLAG: Gerson gives 1656-1717. Reserve: Jan van Huchtenburg (bapt. 20 November 1647 - bur. 2 July 1733; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the battle machine of the network: first taught by Thomas Wijck, then trained in Paris under Adam Frans van der Meulen at the Gobelins, then court painter to Prince Eugene of Savoy with a ten-canvas series of Eugene's battles from Zenta (1697) to Belgrade (1717), plus William III's campaigns. He taught Dirk Maas, collaborated with Jan Wyck, assisted Gerrit Berckheyde by painting his people and horses, and his pupils Alexander van Gaelen and Izaak Vogelensanck carried the Wouwerman battle style into England. He is the reserve because his mechanism set (the monumental multi-figure battle composition, the court-painter campaign series) is the largest scale in the network, a strong contrast study whenever the chain wants one. The doorway is the court painter who turned ten of Prince Eugene's battles into one series. Alternates if any of the 4 fails later (all verified NOT STUDIED): Jan Wyck (29 October 1645 - 17 May 1702; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the son of Thomas Wijck who carried the Haarlem line to England and founded British military and sporting painting: over 150 works, the Battle of the Boyne (1690) painted half a dozen times, William III's campaigns through the Nine Years' War, and as a teacher the master of John Wootton and Martin Beckman. He accompanied Dirk Maas to Ireland to paint William's campaigns, and his battle pieces bring the viewer into the fight at eye level instead of the prevailing bird's-eye disposition of troops. He is an alternate rather than a main because the date conflict needs settling before he can be a top pick. The doorway is the Haarlem battle painter who founded British sporting art from Mortlake. FLAG: dates badly conflicted - infobox 29 October 1645, body 29 October 1652, Gerson 1652-1700, ODNB c. 1645-1700, Government Art Collection c. 1640-1702; the 1676 marriage certificate ("about 31") supports c. 1645, which is used here. Jacob Gole (1660-1737; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the print hop: he worked with Dusart, became an early practitioner of mezzotint, published Dusart's own mezzotints (the Communia Gaudia series including The Patriotic Peasant, c. 1695) in Amsterdam, and engraved the portrait of Adriaen van Ostade after Dusart - so he is the man who carried both Dusart and Ostade into print. His series of the Five Senses, the Seven Virtues, the Greek and Roman goddesses and the Seven Continents give a study a printmaking mechanism set (mezzotint tone, the publisher's series) that no painter in the chain so far has offered. The doorway is the engraver who published Dusart's mezzotints and engraved Ostade after him. Spares (verified, NOT part of the 7 below): Egbert van Heemskerck the Elder (1634 - c. 1702/04; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Haarlem genre painter who took the Ostade-Brouwer low life to London and turned it into satire: taverns, peasant cottages, surgical theatres, backgammon players, Quaker meetings, under the patronage of John Wilmot, 2nd Earl of Rochester. Thought to have trained with Jan Miense Molenaer (already carried as a #535 main), travelled to Italy 1655-1661, and is constantly confused with his son of the same name. His is the study of the peasant tavern become English satire. The doorway is the Haarlem satirist who took the tavern to London and Rochester. Evert Collier (26 January 1642 - few days before 8 September 1708; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the vanitas and trompe-l'oeil painter of the Haarlem guild register: probably pupil of Vincent van der Vinne, guild 1664 (the same impossible-1646 register page as Oudendijck and Heemskerck), then Leiden, Amsterdam and London from 1693. His letter racks, skulls, pocket watches, globes and hourglasses are the still-life answer to Dusart's peasant: the same Haarlem, the opposite subject, and the same London emigration as Wyck and Heemskerck. The doorway is the letter-rack painter who shared Oudendijck's impossible guild date. Adriaen Oudendijck (1677, Haarlem - 1704, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the copycat son: second son of Evert, he copied his cattle after Adriaen van de Velde and his figures after Thomas Wyck, earning the nickname "Rapianus" from Houbraken; worked with Dirk Maas, possibly in the same studio, 1695-1700; guild 1700; died in 1704, the same year as Dusart. He is the study of second-handedness at one remove from Dusart's own copying of Ostade - what it means to build a career out of other men's cattle and figures. The doorway is the copycat who died the same year as Dusart. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#535. Jacob Jordaens (19 May 1593 - 18 October 1678; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Flemish giant behind van der Cooghen: pupil of Adam van Noort (his only teacher, whose daughter he married), never went to Italy, the third of the Antwerp trio after Rubens and Van Dyck, painter of history, genre and tapestry cartoons, collaborator on the Oranjezaal at Huis ten Bosch where van der Cooghen may have trained with him. He is the biggest name in the spares and the farthest step sideways; he is a spare, not a main, because the hop runs through a single pupil (van der Cooghen) and his scale would swamp the Haarlem chain. The doorway is the Antwerp master who taught the amateur in Dusart's inventory. Rachel Ruysch (3 June 1664 - 12 October 1750; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the flower painter of the network: apprenticed at 15 to Willem van Aelst until his death in 1683, married the portraitist Juriaen Pool in 1693, ten children, court painter to the Elector Palatine Johann Wilhelm, still painting at 83. Her link to Dusart runs through Michiel van Musscher (already carried as a #535 alternate), who painted her 1692 portrait in collaboration with her - she painted the flowers, he painted her at work in her studio. She is the second woman in the chain after Judith Leyster, and the first whose career outlasted the whole Haarlem generation by half a century. The doorway is the flower painter Musscher painted at work in her own studio. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#535. Jan van Gool (1685-1763; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the biographer who wrote Dusart's life: pupil of Simon van der Does and Mattheus Terwesten, Hague Drawing School director 1720-1734, author of the Nieuwe Schouburg (1750) as an update to Houbraken, with Dusart on p. 457 and the note that his folios were "appreciated by art collectors who conserved them very carefully". A study of van Gool would be a study of how Dusart was remembered, parallel to the Houbraken spare in #535 - and of the Hague Italianate landscape he painted himself. The doorway is the biographer who wrote Dusart's life and kept his folios in mind. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#535. Gerrit Claesz Bleker (1592 - 8 February 1656; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Haarlem landscape painter of historical allegories: guild before 1643, probably pupil of Nicolaes Moeyaert, teacher of David Decker (whose only known work was long attributed to Cornelis Decker, already carried as a #535 spare) and of Dirck Bleker, and thought to have taught the young Paulus Potter in the mid-1630s. He is the landscape answer to Dusart's cottage interior, one generation up. The doorway is the Haarlem allegorist who may have taught the young Paulus Potter. John Wootton (c. 1682 - 13 November 1764; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the English end of the Wyck line: apprenticed to Jan Wyck in the late 1690s at Mortlake, then the first English painter of horse portraits and the founder of English sporting painting at Newmarket, with patrons from Queen Anne to the Duke of Marlborough and Sir Robert Walpole. His hunting scenes carry Wyck's influence more than his horse portraits do, and he influenced George Stubbs and Thomas Gainsborough. He is the study of a Dutch import become an English institution. The doorway is the page boy who became the father of English sporting art. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#535. (The 15 names carried in #535 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Frans Hals (c. 1582 - 26 August 1666), Jan Miense Molenaer (1610 - buried 19 September 1668), Judith Leyster (bapt. 28 July 1609 - 10 February 1660) (mains); Dirck Hals (19 March 1591 - 17 May 1656) (reserve); Richard Brakenburg (bapt. 22 May 1650 - 28 December 1702), Michiel van Musscher (bapt. 27 January 1645 - 20 June 1705) (alternates); spares: Joos van Craesbeeck (c. 1605/06 - c. 1660), Hendrik Mommers (bapt. 2 January 1620 - buried 21 December 1693), Jacob Duck (c. 1600 - buried 28 January 1667), Cornelis Gerritsz Decker (1618-1678), Frans Pietersz de Grebber (1573-1649), Johannes Cornelisz Verspronck (1597-1662), Jan de Bray (d. 1697), Arnold Houbraken (28 March 1660 - 14 October 1719). Frans Hals (c. 1582 - 26 August 1666; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Ostade's teacher and the root of the whole Haarlem line: the loose, visible brushstroke, the fleeting expression, the merry company, the tronie. He taught or shaped Ostade, Brouwer, Molenaer, Leyster, Verspronck, Roestraten and van der Vinne, and his five painter sons carried the name into the next generation. His late monochrome regent pieces and his 19th-century rehabilitation (Thore-Burger, the Impressionists, van Gogh's praise) give a study a second arc beyond the teaching link. He is main 2 rather than top pick because the chain has just arrived at Ostade from below (the Boths, Bloemaert) and Dusart continues it forward, while Hals is a step back up the tree. The doorway is the teacher whose loose brush Ostade learned from and then left behind. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions of the exact phrase "Frans Hals"; no local file; not carried in #520-#534. Jan Miense Molenaer (1610 - buried 19 September 1668; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the fellow pupil who shared Hals's studio with Ostade and then shared a studio and a marriage with Judith Leyster, and the hinge between Hals and Jan Steen: his style is called a precursor to Steen's, his early work is close to Hals and his later work close to Ostade, and his Five Senses series of 1637 is the most overt visual humour of the Dutch Golden Age. He also brings the literary strand (proverbs and poems by Jacob Cats and Bredero) that Ostade never used. The doorway is the fellow pupil who married the other fellow pupil and became the forerunner of Jan Steen. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#534. FLAG: the Hals article's influence list gives his birth as 1609; his own article gives 1610. Judith Leyster (bapt. 28 July 1609 - 10 February 1660; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the woman of the Hals circle: possibly taught by Frans Pietersz de Grebber, possibly a pupil of Hals, a Guild member at 24 (1633), running her own workshop with pupils, suing Hals himself over a stolen apprentice, then marrying Molenaer in 1636. Her entire oeuvre - only eighteen works attributed with certainty, nearly all from 1628-1635 - was filed under Hals's or her husband's name for close to 200 years until Hofstede de Groot's rediscovery in 1893, when a "Frans Hals" in the Louvre turned out to be signed with her JL-star monogram. She is the strongest non-pupil hop from Ostade and the only woman in the immediate network. The doorway is the woman of the Hals circle whose whole oeuvre was filed under her husband's name for two centuries. Reserve: Dirck Hals (19 March 1591 - 17 May 1656; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Frans's younger brother and the inventor, with Willem Buytewech and Esaias van de Velde, of the merry-company picture: small, colourful, fashionable young people at music, cards and conversation, painted from about 1618-1620 to the end of his life, with a late Leiden period (1641-1648) and a collaboration line with Dirck van Delen. He influenced Nathaniel Bacon, Johann Hulsman and Willem Cornelisz Duyster, and his son Anthonie carried the name on. He is the reserve because his mechanism set (the polite merry company) is the mirror image of Ostade's peasant tavern, which makes him a strong contrast study whenever the chain wants one. The doorway is the younger brother who turned the merry company into a genre of its own. Alternates if any of the 4 fails later (all verified NOT STUDIED): Richard Brakenburg (bapt. 22 May 1650 - 28 December 1702; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the pupil who imitated the wrong master: taught by Ostade (and Hendrik Mommers) but, per Hofstede de Groot, he imitated Jan Steen more closely than either teacher, in style, character types and the arrangement of weddings, merrymakings and quack-doctors, with figures repeated over and over and little invention of his own. His pupil Hendrik de Valk had less still. He is the study of second-handedness itself: what happens when a painter copies the copyist, and where repetition stops being a style. The doorway is the pupil who copied Steen more faithfully than his own master. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#534. Michiel van Musscher (bapt. 27 January 1645 - 20 June 1705; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the pupil with the most documented education in the network: he kept his own record - two months with Martinus Saeghmolen (1660), Abraham van den Tempel (1661), seven lessons with Gabriel Metsu (1665), three months with Ostade in Haarlem (1667) - then became Amsterdam's sought-after portraitist, painting Tsar Peter the Great and the Russian Great Embassy of 1697, and teaching Ottmar Elliger the Younger and Dirk Valkenburg. He is the study of the pupil who outgrew the peasant genre entirely and took Ostade's characterisation into high portraiture. The doorway is the pupil who took seven lessons from Metsu, three months from Ostade, and became Amsterdam's portraitist. His pupils Adriaen Rombouts and Lucas Viters extend the chain one more step. The doorway is the baker of the Antwerp citadel who learned to paint from a prisoner. The doorway is the Haarlem market painter who taught Dirk Maas and travelled to Italy with Berchem. Jacob Duck (c. 1600 - buried 28 January 1667; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the goldsmith turned painter: master goldsmith in Utrecht in 1619, drawing lessons from Droochsloot from 1621, active in Haarlem 1636-1646 alongside Ostade, and the central painter of the guardroom (kortegaardje) and the brothel scene, blending Utrecht Caravaggist humour with Haarlem merry-company painting. His is the study of the soldier's tavern where Ostade painted the peasant's. The doorway is the goldsmith turned guardroom painter who learned drawing from Droochsloot. He is the landscape answer to Ostade's cottage interior. The doorway is the Haarlem landscapist who painted in the manner of Ruisdael and shared Ostade's streets. The doorway is the Haarlem workshop master who may have taught Leyster before Hals did. Johannes Cornelisz Verspronck (1597-1662; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the neat portraitist of Haarlem: son of the portraitist Cornelis Engelsz, one of about ten competing Haarlem portraitists, possibly a pupil of Hals, and the representative of the "neat" school against which Hals's loose painterly style is defined in the Hals article's opening. He is the control case for Hals. The doorway is the neat portraitist against whom Hals's loose brush is measured. Arnold Houbraken (28 March 1660 - 14 October 1719; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - not a genre painter but the network's memory: his Groote Schouburgh (1718-1721) is the source for Ostade as Hals's pupil, the Lubeck flight, the Sennepart stay and the Witsen drawings, and for Brouwer, Molenaer, Musscher, Mommers and half the network above. A study of Houbraken would be a study of how the Dutch Golden Age was remembered, including his errors (Brouwer's birthplace, Hals as his master, Mommers's death year). Jan Steen (1626-1679; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Leiden genre painter; drew technical knowledge from Jan van Goyen, Nicolaus Knupfer and Adriaen van Ostade (Ostade's pupil, per the Ostade page read end to end by leg 3). The great comic moralist of Dutch peasant life - the tavern, the household in disorder - a direct downstream heir of the Brouwer/Ostade low-life line Andries belongs to. The doorway is the comic moralist who turned the peasant tavern into a sermon. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#533. Paulus Potter (bapt. 20 November 1625 - bur. 17 January 1654; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - animal and landscape painter; The Bull (1647, Mauritshuis), painted at 22, made the animal piece a heroic genre; about 100 paintings in a nine-year career, dead of tuberculosis at 28 - the same brief, bright arc as Andries (dead at 29/30). The landscape-with-livestock half of the Both brothers' world, from the Dutch side. The doorway is the bull painter who died at twenty-eight after making the animal heroic. Jacob van Ruisdael (1628-1682; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Haarlem landscape painter; teacher of Meindert Hobbema; the great Dutch landscape after the Italianate fashion had run its course - native light, clouds and water-mills instead of Roman gold. The counterweight to Jan Both's Italianate landscape inside the same generation. The doorway is the native light after the Roman gold.
